Active Subsampling for Measurement-Constrained M-Estimation of Individualized Thresholds with High-Dimensional Data
Jingyi Duan Lehao Fu Yang Ning

Abstract
Measurement-constrained problems frequently arise in modern applications such as electronic health record studies. In such problems, despite the availability of large datasets, collecting labeled data can be highly costly or time-consuming, allowing only a small portion of the data to be labeled within a given budget. This raises a critical question: which data points are most beneficial to label given the budget constraint? We study this question in the context of estimating an optimal individualized threshold under a measurement-constrained M-estimation framework. In particular, our goal is to estimate a high-dimensional parameter 𝜽 \bm{\theta} in a linear threshold 𝜽 T ​ 𝒁 \bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z} for a continuous variable X X such that the discrepancy between whether X X exceeds the threshold 𝜽 T ​ 𝒁 \bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z} and a binary outcome Y Y is minimized. In the measurement-constrained setting, we propose a novel K K -step active subsampling algorithm to estimate 𝜽 \bm{\theta} , which iteratively samples the most informative observations in the dataset and solves a regularized M-estimator. Our theoretical analysis reveals a sharp phase transition phenomenon with respect to β \beta , the smoothness of the conditional density of X X given Y Y and 𝒁 \bm{Z} . In particular, for β > ( 1 + 3 ) / 2 \beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2 , we show that the two-step algorithm (with K = 2 K=2 ) yields an estimator with the parametric convergence rate O p ​ ( ( s ​ log ⁡ d / N ) 1 / 2 ) O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{1/2}) in l 2 l_{2} norm, where d d and s s are the dimension and sparsity of 𝜽 \bm{\theta} respectively and N N is the label budget. The rate is strictly faster than the minimax optimal rate O p ​ ( ( s ​ log ⁡ d / N ) β / ( 2 ​ β + 1 ) ) O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}) with N N i.i.d. samples drawn from the population, demonstrating the benefit of active sampling. For 1 < β ≤ ( 1 + 3 ) / 2 1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2 , the estimator from the two-step algorithm is sub-optimal; running K > 2 K>2 steps recovers the parametric rate. For β ≤ 1 \beta\leq 1 , a super-parametric rate is obtained when K K is scaled properly with N N . Furthermore, we formulate an N N -budget minimax framework for the measurement-constrained M-estimation problem and prove that our estimator is minimax rate optimal up to a logarithmic factor. We also develop Lepski’s methods to achieve adaptation to the unknown smoothness and sparsity respectively, and provide practical guidelines for the implementation of our algorithm. Finally, we demonstrate the superior performance of our method in simulation studies and apply the method to analyze a large diabetes dataset from 130 US hospitals.



Keyword:

Non-regular models, High-dimensional estimation, Sampling, Measurement constraints, Minimax optimality, Kernel smoothing

1
Introduction

In many applications, the scientific questions can be formulated as identifying and interpreting the optimal individualized threshold value for a continuous variable such that the discrepancy with a binary outcome is minimized. One prominent example is the so-called minimum clinically important difference (MCID), which has attracted increasing interests in medical research over the past decade. The MCID is defined as the smallest difference in post-treatment changes, such that a patient is considered experiencing a clinically meaningful improvement
if her/his change exceeds this value. Since introduced by
Jaeschke et al. (1989)
, the MCID has been widely used by clinicians and health policy makers to evaluate the clinical effectiveness of the treatment, because it is tailored to reflect the patient’s satisfaction or the improvement of her/his health condition. More recently, to account for the population heterogeneity, it was suggested by
Hedayat et al. (2015)
;
Zhou et al. (2020)
to incorporate individual patient’s clinical profile to construct the individualized MCID (iMCID).

Formally, let
X
denote a continuous variable representing the measurement of post-treatment change,
Y
denote a binary outcome in
\{-1,+1\}
, where
Y=+1
if the patient’s health condition is improved after receiving the treatment and
Y=-1
otherwise, and
\bm{Z}
denote a vector of
d
-dimensional covariates such as the patient’s demographic information.
Zhou et al. (2020)
defined the iMCID as a function
c(\bm{Z})
which minimizes

\mathbb{P}\left(X<c(\bm{Z})\mid Y=1\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(X>c(\bm{Z})\mid Y=-1\right).

(1.1)

In other words,
c(\bm{Z})
is the optimal individualized threshold for
X
which minimizes the disagreement between the estimated patient’s health condition and the binary outcome
Y
. The optimization problem (
1.1
) provides a unified framework for a wide range of applications, including the estimation of optimal policy in causal inference
(
Zhao et al., 2012
)
, semiparametric binary choice models in econometrics
(
Manski, 1975
)
, and high-dimensional classification in machine learning. We will explore the connections to these and other examples in greater detail in Section
A.1
.

From a practical standpoint, a linear structure on the threshold
c(\bm{Z})
is favored for its transparency and ease of interpretation, particularly when dealing with high-dimensional covariates
\bm{Z}
. In this paper, we assume that
c(\bm{Z})=\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}
for some high-dimensional parameter
\bm{\theta}
. Under these assumptions, we can reformulate (
1.1
) as the following M-estimation problem

\bm{\theta}^{*}=\underset{{\bm{\theta}}}{\operatorname{argmin}}~R(\bm{\theta}),\text{\ where\ }R(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(Y)L_{01}\{Y(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})\}\right],

(1.2)

L_{01}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\{1-\operatorname{sign}(u)\}
is the 0-1 loss, with
\operatorname{sign}(u)=1
if
u\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise, and
\gamma(\cdot)
is a user-specified weight function. While this model-free formulation offers greater generality and potential for accommodating model misspecifications, minimization of the empirical version of
R(\bm{\theta})
is computationally NP-hard due to the 0-1 loss. Additionally, it is well known that the M-estimator of (
1.2
) is non-regular, resulting in non-standard limiting distributions and rates of convergence
(
Kim and Pollard, 1990
)
. Recently,
Feng et al. (2022)
;
Feng et al. (2024)
proposed a regularized M-estimation framework with a smoothed surrogate loss to estimate and make inference on the high-dimensional parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. In particular, they demonstrated that the convergence rate for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}
in
l_{2}
norm is given by
(s\log d/N)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}
, where
d
and
s
are the dimension and sparsity of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
respectively,
N
is the sample size, and
\beta\geq 1
is the smoothness of the conditional density of
X
given the response
Y
and the covariates
\bm{Z}
. With the slower-than-classic root-
N
rate, they also established that the resulting estimator is minimax rate optimal up to a logarithmic factor.

Up to this point, all aforementioned methods rely on the assumptions that the observations are i.i.d. and that the estimation process does not influence the data collection process. However, in practice, these two processes are often intertwined, especially under the measurement-constrained setting, where
(X,\bm{Z})
are observed for all samples, but the response variable
Y
can be measured only for a small subset
(
Wang et al., 2017
;
Zhang et al., 2021
)
. Such constraints arise whenever labeling is expensive or time-consuming. For instance, in EHR (electronic health records) studies, while the database may contain rich patient-level covariates
(X,\bm{Z})
, the gold-standard outcome
Y
often requires manual chart reviews from medical experts. Due to very expensive cost, only a carefully sampled subset of patients can be labeled, and the sampling scheme clearly affects the accuracy of the corresponding M-estimator.

To address this issue, we propose a novel
K
-step active subsampling algorithm for estimating the high-dimensional parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in (
1.2
) under the following measurement-constrained setting. Formally, assume that we have access to a very large unlabeled dataset (e.g., the EHR database)
D=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
n
i.i.d. samples, where the outcome
Y_{i}
is unavailable. Let
N
denote the label budget, that is the expected total number of samples we are allowed to select. Once a specific data point is sampled, we can observe the outcome
Y_{i}
(e.g., via chart reviews in the EHR studies). Our goal is to design a computationally and statistically efficient interactive sampling and estimation procedure for
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, subject to the budget constraint
N
, in the scenario
N\ll n
and
N\ll d
. Our proposed algorithm begins by uniformly sampling a set of independent data from
D
, and solving a regularized M-estimator with a smoothed surrogate loss to construct an initial estimator for
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. We then iteratively use the estimator from the previous iteration to guide the selection of a new set of independent samples and solve the corresponding regularized M-estimator. Repeating this process
K
times yields our final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
.
The underlying principle behind our algorithm is that, as the algorithm iterates, the M-estimators lead to a sequence of intervals with decreasing lengths that contain the true threshold
\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}
with high probability, and in return sampling data in the corresponding neighborhoods around the threshold can further improve the estimation accuracy of our M-estimators.
Importantly, the probability of the
i
th data point being sampled only depends on
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
and the previously sampled data, making it applicable to the measurement-constrained setting. Computationally, by leveraging the smoothness of the surrogate loss, we can design gradient-based
algorithms to solve the regularized M-estimator at each iteration. As a result, our proposed
K
-step active subsampling algorithm is computationally efficient.

To investigate the theoretical results of our estimators, we assume that the conditional density of
X
given
Y
and
\bm{Z}
satisfies the Hölder smoothness condition with parameter
\beta
. Our analysis reveals a sharp phase transition with respect to
\beta
. In particular, for
\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, with a proper choice of tuning parameters, the two-step algorithm, i.e., our algorithm with only
K=2
iterations, attains the parametric rate
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}=O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{1/2})
, where
d
and
s
are the dimension and sparsity of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
respectively and
N
is the label budget. The rate is strictly faster than the minimax optimal rate
O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)})
with
N
i.i.d. samples drawn from the population
(
Feng et al., 2022
)
. In other words, the two-step algorithm requires less data to attain the same order of convergence rate. However, for
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, the estimator from the two-step algorithm is sub-optimal. To achieve the same parametric rate
O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{1/2})
, we need to run at least
K=\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})\rceil+1
iterations in our algorithm, where
K
is strictly greater than 2 but is fixed and finite. For the last case
\beta\leq 1
,
we can achieve a super-parametric rate
O_{p}((s\log d/N)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}})
up to logarithmic factors with
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
iterations, where
K
diverges slowly as
N
tends to infinity. The distinct regimes reflect how quickly the sequence of estimators enters a fast convergence neighborhood of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
.

Moreover, we formulate a minimax framework to study the optimality of our estimators. Unlike the traditional minimax framework, the distribution of a generic estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
depends on the joint distribution
P
of
(X,\bm{Z},Y)
as well as the sampling distribution
Q
, where
P
is determined by the nature but we have the freedom to choose
Q
. After introducing two proper classes
\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
for
P
and
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
for
Q
, we define the
N
-budget minimax risk in
l_{2}
norm as

\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))}\inf_{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2},

where the supremum is only for the distribution
P
in
\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
, the inner infimum is over all possible estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
based on the observed data and the outer infimum is over all possible sampling distributions
Q
in
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
, which contains necessary constraints on
Q
such as the conditional independence assumptions for the sampling mechanism and the budget constraint. We prove in Theorem
5
that the
N
-budget minimax risk for estimating
\bm{\theta}
in
l_{2}
norm is lower bounded by
(s\log(d/s)/N)^{\frac{1}{2(\beta\wedge 1)}}
, which implies that our proposed estimators are indeed rate optimal up to logarithmic factors in the measurement-constrained setting. Finally, we also develop Lepski’s methods to achieve adaptation to the unknown
smoothness
\beta
and sparsity
s
respectively.

1.1
Related Work

Subsampling is an effective method for handling computational constraints when dealing with massive datasets.
There’s a large literature on subsampling algorithms for regression models, such as linear regression
(
Drineas et al., 2011
;
Ma et al., 2014
;
Wang et al., 2019
;
Raskutti and Mahoney, 2016
)
and generalized linear models
(
Wang et al., 2018
)
. Given the budget constraint, the goal is to construct an estimator based on the sampled data to approximate the least squares or the maximum likelihood estimator from the entire dataset, and find the optimal subsampling weight by minimizing the asymptotic variance. The similar idea has been extended to deal with the measurement-constrained problems
(
Wang et al., 2017
;
Zhang et al., 2021
)
. More recently,
Zrnic and Candès (2024)
proposed to use a machine learning model to identify which data points are most beneficial to label, and then find the optimal sampling weight by minimizing the variance of the estimator or classification uncertainty.
For binary data, the case-control subsampling is considered by
Fithian and Hastie (2014)
among many others.

In recent years, substantial research has focused on adaptive experimental design, often aimed at efficiently estimating average treatment effects.
i
Hadad et al. (2021)
considered how to construct confidence intervals for the average treatment effect with adaptively collected data. A recent overview of adaptive design is given by
Perera et al. (2020)
.

All aforementioned works share the similarity that the data are collected adaptively to improve asymptotic efficiency of the standard root-
N
statistical inference. However, our work focuses on the threshold estimation problem which is known as a non-regular problem with rate slower than root-
N
. Our goal is to design a subsampling procedure to accelerate the convergence rate. Thus, both the method and its theory are fundamentally different from the prior literature.

Another closely related area is active learning, see
Balcan et al. (2007)
;
Koltchinskii (2010)
;
Balcan and Long (2013)
;
Castro and Nowak (2008)
;
Wang and Singh (2016)
, among many others.
While our problem setup is similar to the pool-based active learning algorithms, our work is distinct from the active learning literature in both method and theory. Specifically, our algorithm iteratively solves regularized M-estimators with a smoothed surrogate loss via gradient-based methods, which is computationally efficient. However, most of the margin-based active learning algorithms such as

Balcan et al. (2007)
;
Wang and Singh (2016)
require to minimize the empirical 0-1 loss, which is computationally intractable especially in high-dimensional setting. In addition, when the smoothness parameter
\beta
is greater than
(1+\sqrt{3})/2\approx 1.37
, our proposed algorithm employs a streamlined two-step process (i.e.,
K=2
iterations), which significantly enhances implementation simplicity and efficiency. In contrast, the pipeline of a typical active learning algorithm often involves iterative model updates (usually
\log N
iterations) until a stopping criterion is met. In theory, the Tsybakov noise condition plays a pivotal role in deriving theoretical guarantees in the active learning literature.
Technically, when establishing the theoretical guarantees, the stopping criteria and the number of total iterations in the active learning algorithm are determined to ensure compliance with the assumed Tsybakov noise condition. In contrast, our analysis relies on the smoothness of the conditional density of
X
given
\bm{Z}
and
Y
rather than such noise conditions, and our estimator exhibits a sharp phase transition in
\beta
that has no counterpart in the active learning literature. Consequently, the two lines of work adopt different algorithms, rely on different structures, and yield distinct theoretical results and proof strategies.

This work is also related to semi-supervised inference, where we observe a set of labeled data together with a set of unlabeled data
(
Cai and Guo, 2020
;
Zhang and Bradic, 2022
;
Azriel et al., 2022
;
Deng et al., 2024
)
. Under the assumption that the covariates in both labeled and unlabeled samples follow the same distribution, the labeled data can be viewed as being uniformly sampled from the combined dataset in a measurement-constrained setting. Therefore, in semi-supervised inference, the sampling scheme is fixed and known, and the goal is to develop an inference procedure with improved statistical efficiency by leveraging information from the unlabeled data. In contrast, our problem involves designing not only an estimation procedure but also a subsampling algorithm to attain estimation optimality.

1.2
Organization of the paper

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces our proposed active subsampling algorithm and the corresponding estimator. Section
3
derives upper bounds on the estimation error and establishes a matching minimax lower bound. Section
4
discusses the practical implementation and provides a data-driven version of the procedure. Simulation studies and a real data application are presented in Sections
5
and
6
, respectively.

1.3
Notations

We write
\ind\{\}
for the indicator function. For any set
S
, we write
|S|
for its cardinality.
For
\bm{v}=\left(v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\right)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\bm{v}_{S}
to denote the subvector of
\bm{v}
with entries indexed by
the set
S
.
For
q=[1,\infty)
,
\|\bm{v}\|_{q}=(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left|v_{i}\right|^{q})^{1/q}
and
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\ind\{v_{i}\neq 0\}
. For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
. For any positive sequences
\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\}
and
\{b_{1},b_{2},\ldots\}
, we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
or
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if there exists a constant
c
such that
a_{n}\leq cb_{n}
for any
n
, and
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
. Let
\lfloor a\rfloor
be the greatest integer strictly less than
a
, and
\lceil a\rceil
be the smallest integer strictly greater than
a
. Let
\lambda_{\min}(M)
and
\lambda_{\max}(M)
be the smallest and largest eigenvalues of
M
.
A random variable
X
is called sub-Gaussian if there exists a positive constant
K
such that
\mathbb{P}(|X|\geq t)\leq 2\exp(-t^{2}/K^{2})
for all
t\geq 0
. The sub-Gaussian norm of
X
is defined as
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}=\inf\left\{c>0:\mathbb{E}[\exp(X^{2}/c^{2})]\leq 2\right\}
.
A vector
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a sub-Gaussian vector if the one-dimensional marginals
\bm{v}^{T}\bm{X}
are sub-Gaussian for all
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and its sub-Gaussian norm is defined as
\|\bm{X}\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1}\left\|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{X}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

2
Proposed Method

2.1
Background and Heuristics for Subsampling

In this section, we briefly review the regularized M-estimation approach proposed by
Feng et al. (2022)
for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}
in (
1.2
) and use it to motivate our subsampling strategy. For now, assume that we observe
n
i.i.d copies of
(X,\bm{Z},Y)
. Recall that the risk function
R(\bm{\theta})
is defined in (
1.2
). While
R(\bm{\theta})
is typically a smooth function of the parameter
\bm{\theta}
, the empirical version
R_{n}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma(Y_{i})L_{01}\{Y_{i}(X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i})\}
is non-smooth. Minimizing
R_{n}(\bm{\theta})
is computationally intractable when
d
is large and yields the cube root asymptotics
(
Kim and Pollard, 1990
)
.

To address these challenges,
Feng et al. (2022)
proposed to approximate the 0-1 loss by the following smoothed surrogate loss

L_{\delta}(u)=\int_{u/\delta}^{\infty}K(t)dt,

(2.1)

where
K(t)
is a proper kernel function defined in Assumption
3.4
, and
\delta>0
is a bandwidth parameter. As the bandwidth
\delta\rightarrow 0
, we have
L_{\delta}(u)\rightarrow L_{01}(u)
for any
u\neq 0
. Thus, it is intuitive to estimate
\bm{\theta}^{*}
by the minimizer of the regularized smoothed empirical risk,
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{iid}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}\{R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}
, where

R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma(Y_{i})L_{\delta}(Y_{i}(X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i})),

and
\lambda
is a tuning parameter. While
R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta})
is still non-convex,
Feng et al. (2022)
showed that the entire solution
path for the lasso type estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{iid}
can be computed efficiently via the path-following algorithm. In addition, with a proper choice of
\delta
and
\lambda
, the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{iid}
is faster than the classic cube root rate.

By the M-estimation theory, since
R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta})
is differentiable in
\bm{\theta}
, the gradient of
R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta})
at
\bm{\theta}^{*}
,

\nabla R^{n}_{\delta,iid}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma(Y_{i})\frac{Z_{i}Y_{i}}{\delta}K\Big(\frac{Y_{i}(X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}_{i})}{\delta}\Big),

together with some curvature conditions, determine the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{iid}
. A key observation that inspires our subsampling method is that, by the property of the kernel function
K(\cdot)
, the gradient assigns larger weight to observations whose linearized residual
X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}_{i}
is closer to zero. These “near-threshold” points are therefore the most informative for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, and should be prioritized for labeling in our subsampling algorithm.

Algorithm 1

\bm{\theta}\leftarrow

K
-step Active Subsampling

input:

D=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, label budget
N
, the number of iterations
K

parameter:

\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K},\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1},\{\delta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and
\{N_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{K}N_{k}=N

Randomly split
D
into
K
batches:
D_{1},\cdots,D_{K}
, each with batch size
n/K
.

Draw data
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
from
D_{1}
with probability
c_{n,1}=N_{1}K/n
. Acquire the label
Y_{i}
for each sampled data and form the dataset
D_{1}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, where
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{1}
.

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{1}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}
.

for

k=2
to
K

do

Compute the active set:
S_{k}\leftarrow\Big\{(X,\bm{Z}):-b_{k-1}\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}}\leq b_{k-1}\Big\}
.

Given
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{k}
, draw the data point
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
from
D_{k}
with probability
c_{n,k}=N_{k}K/\left(n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)\right)
. Acquire the label
Y_{i}
for each sampled data and form
D_{k}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, where
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}
.

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{k}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}
.

end

for

return

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}

2.2
Active Subsampling Algorithm

Now, let us consider the measurement-constrained setting. Recall that we observe a very large unlabeled dataset
D=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
with
n
i.i.d. samples, where the outcome
Y_{i}
is unavailable. We seek to sample
N\ll n
data points (on average) from the dataset
D
and collect their outcomes to construct an optimal estimator of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
.

We introduce a binary random variable
R_{i}
with
R_{i}=1
if
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
is sampled and
R_{i}=0
otherwise. Our active subsampling approach is outlined in Algorithm
1
. The algorithm runs for a total of
K
iterations, where
K
is to be specified later on. To keep the data distribution during each iteration identical to the original data
D
, we randomly divide
D
into
K
batches
D_{1},\cdots,D_{K}
with equal size
n/K
.
In the first iteration, since there is no prior information on
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, we uniformly sample data from
D_{1}
with probability
0<c_{n,1}<1
. That is, for each
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{1}
,
R_{i}
is generated independently with probability

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1)=c_{n,1}=\frac{N_{1}K}{n},

(2.2)

where
N_{1}
is the expected number of data points sampled in the first iteration. Given the sampled dataset
D_{1}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, we then minimize the regularized smoothed empirical risk function to obtain

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{1}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\},

(2.3)

where
R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta})
is a special case of (
2.4
) with a bandwidth parameter
\delta_{1}
and
\lambda_{1}>0
is a regularization parameter. In general, with the sampled data from the dataset
D_{k}
with
|D_{k}|=n/K
, we define

R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{K}{n}\sum_{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}}\gamma(Y_{i})L_{\delta_{k}}(Y_{i}(X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}))R_{i},

(2.4)

for all
1\leq k\leq K
. For the iteration
2\leq k\leq K
, we define an active set

S_{k}:=\left\{(X,\bm{Z})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:-b_{k-1}\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}}\leq b_{k-1}\right\},

(2.5)

where
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
is the estimator derived from the
(k-1)
th iteration, and
b_{k-1}>0
is the tuning parameter controlling the size of
S_{k}
. Then we uniformly sample the data points in
D_{k}
which fall within the active set
S_{k}
. Specifically, given
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we generate
R_{i}
from a Bernoulli distribution with

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})=c_{n,k}\cdot\ind\{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{k}\},

(2.6)

where
c_{n,k}=N_{k}K/\left(n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)\right)
enforces the label budget constraint
N_{k}
for this iteration. The sampling mechanism implies that only points whose estimated residual
X_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\bm{Z}_{i}
is sufficiently close to 0 are potentially sampled, aligning with the intuition in Section
2.1
.

Given how
R_{i}
is generated, we can verify that the independence assumptions
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\perp\bar{H}_{i-1}
and
R_{i}\perp Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1}
hold, where
\bar{H}_{i-1}
denotes all observed data right before we decide whether
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
is sampled or not. These two independence assumptions play an important role in the minimax lower bound; see Section
3.3
for more detailed discussions.

Our
k
-step estimator is

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{k}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\},

(2.7)

where
R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
is defined in (
2.4
). Repeating this procedure
K
times, we obtain our final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
.

In our algorithm, the sampling probability
c_{n,k}
, which depends on
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)
, is assumed to be known. In practice, provided the active set
S_{k}
is given, we can estimate
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)
easily from a large amount of unlabeled data. Specifically, in Algorithm
1
, we can instead randomly divide the dataset
D
into
2K-1
batches
D_{1},D_{20},D_{21},\cdots,D_{K0},D_{K1}
, where we use
D_{k0}
to compute an empirical estimator
\widehat{p}_{k}
of
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)
, and then construct a plug-in estimator
\widehat{c}_{n,k}:=N_{k}K/(n\widehat{p}_{k})
of
c_{n,k}
.
We draw samples from
D_{k1}
according to (
2.6
) with
c_{n,k}
replaced by
\widehat{c}_{n,k}
and compute the estimator in (
2.7
). The sample splitting strategy guarantees the desired independence when deriving the theoretical properties of the estimators; see Section
A.2
for the theoretical results.
Further computational details are given in Section
4
.

3
Theoretical Results

We first list the technical assumptions in Section
3.1
, then derive the convergence rate of our estimator in Section
3.2
and establish the minimax lower bound in Section
3.3
. Without loss of generality, we set the weight function in (
1.2
) to
\gamma(y)=1/\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
.

3.1
Assumptions

Assumption 3.1
.

\bm{\theta}^{*}
is
s
-sparse with
\left\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\right\|_{0}\leq s

and
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C
for some constant
C
.

Assumption
3.1
quantifies the properties of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. In addition to the sparsity of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, we impose the bound on
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
, so that
the threshold
\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}
has the same scale as
X
. Technically, this condition is used to verify the restricted strong convexity (RSC) condition
(
Feng et al., 2022
)
. In particular, we give an example in Section
A.9
showing that the RSC condition (such as Assumption
3.5
below) fails if
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\rightarrow\infty
.

Assumption 3.2
.

(i) There exists a constant
0<c<1/2
such that
c\leq\mathbb{P}(Y=1)\leq 1-c
.

(ii) Assume that
\left|Z_{ij}\right|\leq M_{n}
for any
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq d
, where
M_{n}
is allowed to increase with
n
such that

M_{n}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{n\min_{1\leq k\leq K}\delta_{k}c_{n,k}}{K\log d}}

(3.1)

for some constant
C
, where
K
is the number of iterations,
\delta_{k}
is the bandwidth parameter in the
k
th iteration and

c_{n,k}
is defined in (
2.6
).
In addition, it holds that

\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y\right)\bm{v}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq M_{1},

(3.2)

for some constant
M_{1}>0
, where
s^{\prime}=Cs
for some sufficiently large constant
C
.

(iii)
\bm{Z}
given
Y=y
is a sub-Gaussian vector with a bounded sub-Gaussian norm.

Part (i) ensures that the weight function
\gamma(y)=1/\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
is bounded away from infinity. For part (ii), if each component of
\bm{Z}
is sub-Gaussian with bounded sub-Gaussian norm,
\max_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq d}\left|Z_{ij}\right|\leq M_{n}
holds with high probability with
M_{n}\asymp(\log(d\vee n))^{1/2}
. Given the choices of
\delta_{k}
and
c_{n,k}
in Theorem
2
, (
3.1
) reduces to

M_{n}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{N}{K\log d}}
,
which is a mild condition provided
N
is large enough. Furthermore, (
3.2
) controls the maximal sparse eigenvalues of
\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y)
, we refer to
Bühlmann and Van De Geer (2011)
for the detailed discussion. Finally, part (iii) is used to provide a sharp bound for the plug-in error of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
in the active set
S_{k}
. This condition can be removed with the price of obtaining a sub-optimal rate for our estimator; see Section
A.6.1
for the detailed result.

The following definition and assumption quantify the smoothness of the conditional density of
X
given
Y
and
\bm{Z}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
be the greatest integer strictly less than
\beta
. We say
P\in\mathcal{P}\left(\beta,L\right)
if the conditional density
f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
of
X
given
(Y,\bm{Z})
is
l
times differentiable w.r.t
x\in[\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}-c,\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+c]
for an arbitrarily small constant
c>0
, and satisfies

\left|f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\Delta\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right|\leq L\left|\Delta\right|^{\beta-l},

(3.3)

for any
|\Delta|\leq c,y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some constant
L>0
.

Assumption 3.3
.

We assume
P\in\mathcal{P}\left(\beta,L\right)
, where
\beta>0
and
L>0
are constants. In addition,

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})<p_{\max}<\infty,

(3.4)

and there exists a set
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{G})\geq C
for some constant
0<C\leq 1
and

\inf_{x\in B(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z},\epsilon_{n}),\bm{z}\in\mathcal{G}}f(x\mid\bm{z})\geq p_{\min}>0,

(3.5)

where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
are the support sets of
X
and
\bm{Z}
,
B(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z},\epsilon_{n}):=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:|x-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}|\leq\epsilon_{n}\}
,
\epsilon_{n}=C\max_{2\leq k\leq K}b_{k-1}
for some constant
C
large enough,
and

p_{\max},p_{\min}
are positive constants.

Here,
f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is assumed to belong to a
\beta
-smooth Hölder class at the point
x=\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}
. Differentiability of
f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is required only in a small neighborhood of
x=\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}
.
In addition,
f(x\mid y,\bm{z})
is assumed to be upper bounded by a constant. To guarantee that the active set
S_{k}
receives enough probability mass, we further require that on some region
\mathcal{G}
of
\bm{z}
, the density

f(x\mid\bm{z})
is lower bounded by some constant for any
\bm{z}\in\mathcal{G}
and
x\in B(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z},\epsilon_{n})
.

Assumption 3.4
.

Assume that
K(t)
is a proper kernel of order
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
with bounded support, where
\beta
is the smoothness parameter in Assumption
3.3
. That is
K(t)
satisfies
K(t)=K(-t)
,
|K(t)|\leq K_{\max}<\infty
,
\int K(t)dt=1
,
\int K^{2}(t)dt<\infty
,
\int t^{j}K(t)dt=0,\forall j=1,\ldots,l
, and
\int|K(t)||t|^{q}dt
is bounded by a constant for any
q\in[l,l+1]
.

As in classical non-parametric estimation, we adopt a kernel of order
l
to control the higher order bias of the gradient of the smoothed empirical risk
\mathbb{E}(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})
; see Proposition
A.2
. For clarity we present our theoretical results for the kernel with bounded support. With minor changes to the proofs, our results can be extended to kernels with mild tail conditions such as Gaussian kernels. The detailed results are deferred to Section
A.6.1
.

Assumption 3.5
.

There exists a sequence of sets
\Omega_{k-1}
, such that
\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\Omega_{k-1}
and the following restricted strong convexity (RSC) and restricted smoothness (RSM) conditions hold for
R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}\right)
over sparse vectors in
\Omega_{k-1}
, for
1\leq k\leq K
. That is, uniformly over
1\leq k\leq K
and
\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1}
with
(\left\|\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right\|_{0}\vee\|\bm{\theta}\|_{0})\lesssim s
, we have

R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right)\geq R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})^{T}\left(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-}_{n,k}\left\|\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(3.6)

and

R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right)\leq R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})^{T}\left(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{+}_{n,k}\left\|\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(3.7)

where, for some positive constants
C_{1}\leq C_{2}
,

(\rho^{-}_{n,k},\rho^{+}_{n,k})=\begin{cases}(C_{1}c_{n,k},\;C_{2}c_{n,k}),&\text{if }\beta\geq 1,\\[6.0pt]
(C_{1}\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}c_{n,k},\;C_{2}\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}c_{n,k}),&\text{if }0<\beta<1.\end{cases}

The RSC and RSM conditions are commonly used to analyze the statistical rate and computational guarantees for non-convex optimization problems in high-dimensional regression. Similar conditions have been discussed extensively in the literature; see
Bühlmann and Van De Geer (2011)
. In our context, we require that the smoothed empirical risk
R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right)
is
\rho^{-}_{n,k}
-strongly convex in (
3.6
) and
\rho^{+}_{n,k}
-smooth in (
3.7
) when restricted to sparse vectors in
\Omega_{k-1}
, where both
\rho^{-}_{n,k}
and
\rho^{+}_{n,k}
scale with
c_{n,k}
when
\beta\geq 1
and
\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}c_{n,k}
when
0<\beta<1
. The factor
c_{n,k}
appears in the rate of
(\rho^{-}_{n,k},\rho^{+}_{n,k})
because only the selected data points with
R_{i}=1
enter
R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}\right)
. However, the assumed rate of
(\rho^{-}_{n,k},\rho^{+}_{n,k})
differs in terms of whether the smoothness parameter
\beta
exceeds 1 or not. When
\beta>1
, under mild conditions, the population risk function
R(\bm{\theta})
defined in (
1.2
) satisfies

\rho_{-}\leq\lambda_{\min}\!\big(\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\big)\leq\lambda_{\max}\!\big(\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\big)\leq\rho_{+},

(3.8)

for some constants
\rho_{+}>\rho_{-}>0
, so the smoothed empirical curvature is naturally of order
c_{n,k}
. When
0<\beta\leq 1
, the function
R(\bm{\theta})
is not second-order differentiable and instead grows much faster than a quadratic function locally around
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. More precisely, we can show that, when
0<\beta<1
, there exists a small neighborhood
\bar{\Omega}
of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, such that for any
\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\Omega}
,

\rho_{-}\,\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{1+\beta}\leq R(\bm{\theta})-R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\leq\rho_{+}\,\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{1+\beta},

(3.9)

for some constants
\rho_{+}>\rho_{-}>0
. After smoothing, the smoothed empirical risk
R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
is always second-order differentiable, provided that
K^{\prime}(\cdot)
exists. However, the corresponding eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
blow up with the rate
\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}\rightarrow\infty
as
\delta_{k}\rightarrow 0
(ignoring the effect of
c_{n,k}
), which is indeed driven by the geometric structure of
R(\bm{\theta})
in (
3.9
).

Assumption
3.5
as well as (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) can be verified under specific distributional assumptions. The proof is deferred to Section
A.7
.

3.2
Convergence Rate of the Proposed Estimator

We first present a master theorem that characterizes the effect of subsampling on the convergence rate of our estimators at each iteration.

Theorem 1
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
, for any
1\leq k\leq K
, choose
\lambda_{k}\asymp\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}
, and
\delta_{k}\asymp\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,k}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}
. With probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
, we have

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{s\log d}{N_{1}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}},\ \|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N_{1}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}}.

(3.10)

For
2\leq k\leq K
, if we further assume

\displaystyle b_{k-1}\geq C\delta_{k},~~\textrm{the event}~~\mathcal{W}_{k-1}=\Big\{b_{k-1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\frac{N_{k}}{s\log d}}\Big\}~~\textrm{holds}

(3.11)

for some large constant
C>0
and there exists a large constant
\zeta
such that
(\frac{s\log d}{N_{k}})^{\zeta}\lesssim\delta_{k}
with
\delta_{k}=o(1)
, then with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)s\log d}{N_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}},

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)s\log d}{N_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}},

(3.12)

where
N_{k}=\sum_{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left(R_{i}\right)
is the expected sample size at the
k
th iteration.

In this theorem, the tuning parameters
\lambda_{k}
and
\delta_{k}
are determined by a bias-variance trade-off. Specifically, Proposition
A.2
implies that the bias of the smoothed empirical risk
R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
due to kernel smoothing satisfies

\Big\|\mathbb{E}(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})-\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim c_{n,k}\delta_{k}^{\beta}.

Moreover, Proposition
A.1
characterizes the stochastic error of
\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
,

\Big\|\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\mathbb{E}(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}.

The bandwidth
\delta_{k}
needs to be chosen to balance the bias
c_{n,k}\delta_{k}^{\beta}
with the stochastic error
\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}
multiplied by
\sqrt{s}
, which yields
\delta_{k}\asymp\Big(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,k}}\Big)^{1/(2\beta+1)}
. The shrinkage parameter
\lambda_{k}
needs to dominate the stochastic error of
\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
to exploit the sparsity of
\bm{\theta}
. In practice, the tuning parameters can be determined by a cross-validation approach shown in Section
4
.

In the following, we comment on the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
. For
k=1
, the non-standard convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
in (
3.10
) generalizes the results in
Feng et al. (2022)
. In particular, when
\beta\geq 1
, the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
in the
l_{2}
norm is
O_{p}((\frac{s\log d}{N_{1}})^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}})
, which recovers the result obtained by
Feng et al. (2022)
when data are i.i.d drawn from the population. However, their results do not cover the case
0<\beta<1
, as the population risk exhibits the non-quadratic behavior shown in (
3.9
). We close this gap by showing that the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
in the
l_{2}
norm is
O_{p}((\frac{s\log d}{N_{1}})^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}})
for
0<\beta<1
.

For
k\geq 2
, the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
in (
3.12
) contains an additional factor
\mathbb{P}((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k})
. By the definition of the active set
S_{k}
in (
2.5
),
\mathbb{P}((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k})
depends on the regularity of the joint distribution of
(X,\bm{Z})
, the accuracy of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
from the previous iteration and the choice of
b_{k-1}
. Under Assumption
3.3
and when
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
is close enough to
\bm{\theta}^{*}
, we can show that
\mathbb{P}((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k})
is proportional to
b_{k-1}
. If we allow
b_{k-1}\rightarrow 0
and
N_{k}
no smaller than
N_{1}
, the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
obtained via active subsampling is faster than
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
obtained under uniform subsampling. Moreover, since (
3.12
) holds for any choice of
b_{k-1}
provided (
3.11
) is satisfied, there is a trade-off for determining the optimal choice of
b_{k-1}
and the corresponding optimal rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
. Our previous argument suggests that a smaller value of
b_{k-1}
is desirable to attain a faster rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
in (
3.12
). However, condition (
3.11
) prevents us from choosing the value of
b_{k-1}
too small, see Remark
1
below for interpretation of condition (
3.11
). These together yield the optimal choice of
b_{k-1}
and the corresponding optimal rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
.

Remark 1
.

Condition (
3.11
) is inherited from Proposition
A.2
, which controls the approximation error of
\mathbb{E}(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})
to
\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})
(which is 0 by definition). Since we show that
\mathbb{P}((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k})\asymp b_{k-1}
, we can interpret
b_{k-1}
as the size of the active set
S_{k}
. The first condition
b_{k-1}\geq C\delta_{k}
in (
3.11
) requires that the bandwidth
\delta_{k}
should be chosen in a smaller order than the size of the active set. Otherwise, the surrogate risk
R_{\delta}(\cdot)
is not shrunk to
R(\cdot)
sufficiently fast. The event
\mathcal{W}_{k-1}
in (
3.11
) is concerned with the stability of the active set
S_{k}
with respect to the plug-in estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
. Recall that the samples with
|X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}_{i}|\approx 0
are the most informative for estimating
\bm{\theta}
. To keep them inside
S_{k}
despite the plug-in error of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we need
b_{k-1}
to dominate
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
up to some logarithmic factors.

To obtain the optimal rate for our final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
via Theorem
1
, we need to optimize the parameters
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K},\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1},\{\delta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and
\{N_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
as well as the number of iterations
K
in Algorithm
1
. In the following, we show that our algorithm exhibits a phase transition at two critical values 1 and
(1+\sqrt{3})/2
of the smoothness parameter
\beta
. We start from the theoretical result for
\beta\in((1+\sqrt{3})/2,+\infty)
.

Theorem 2

(Optimal rate for
\beta>\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
)
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold,
K\geq 2
and
\beta>\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
are both fixed. We set
N_{k}=N/K
for
1\leq k\leq K
and

\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}},

\delta_{k}=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\ \lambda_{k}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}\delta_{k}}},\ b_{k-1}=c_{3}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},2\leq k\leq K,

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
. If

N\lesssim(s\log d)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}n^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},

(3.13)

and
s\log d=o(N)
, then with probability greater than
1-2K/d
, we have

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2},\ ~~\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2},

(3.14)

uniformly over
2\leq k\leq K
, where
N
is the pre-specified label budget.

The tuning parameters
\delta_{k}
and
\lambda_{k}
are chosen in the same way as in Theorem
1
, where we plug in
c_{n,k}=\frac{KN_{k}}{n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)}
and use the intermediate result
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)\asymp b_{k-1}
. In addition, when
\beta>\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
, we choose the smallest value of
b_{k-1}
such that the condition
b_{k-1}\geq C\delta_{k}
in (
3.11
) holds, and we verify that the event
\mathcal{W}_{k-1}
in (
3.11
) also holds with high probability for such choice of
b_{k-1}
. The condition (
3.13
) guarantees that the active set
S_{k}
contains enough unlabeled data to draw from. In this theorem, the budget
N
is evenly divided across
K
iterations so that the expected sample size at the final iteration is
N_{K}=N/K
. Intuitively, it may be desirable to allocate more budget to compute
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
as
k
increases. However, this does not improve the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
; see Section
A.6.3
for a variant of Theorem
2
in this case.

The convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
at each iterations is shown in (
3.14
). For any
k\geq 2
, the rate can be viewed as the parametric rate for sparse models and is faster than the minimax optimal rate with
N
i.i.d. data
(
Feng et al., 2022
)
, demonstrating the benefit of active sampling. Moreover, as the rate (
3.14
) stays the same for any
k\geq 2
, it suffices to only run
K=2
iterations in Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 3

(Optimal rate for
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
)
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold,
K=\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})\rceil+1
and
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
are fixed. We set
N_{k}=N/K
for
1\leq k\leq K
,

\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}},

for
2\leq k\leq K-1
,

\ b_{k-1}=c_{3}\left(\log(\frac{N}{s\log d})\right)^{\frac{(2\beta+1)(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})}{2(\beta+1)}}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})},

\delta_{k}=c_{1}\left(\frac{b_{k-1}s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{k}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}\delta_{k}}},

and

b_{K-1}=c_{3}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\delta_{K}=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\lambda_{K}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{K-1}\delta_{K}}},

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
. If

N\lesssim\Big(\log(\frac{N}{s\log d})\Big)^{\frac{\beta+1}{2(2\beta+1)}}(s\log d)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}n^{\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta+1}},

(3.15)

(
3.13
) and
s\log d=o(N)
hold, then with probability greater than
1-2K/d
, we have

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2},\ ~~~\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2}.

(3.16)

Compared with Theorem
2
, our estimator behaves differently when
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
. While we still obtain the same parametric rate for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
in (
3.16
), we need to run at least
K=\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})\rceil+1
iterations in Algorithm
1
to attain (
3.16
) as opposed to only
K=2
iterations in Theorem
2
. Note that
\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})
is well defined and is strictly greater than 1 for
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
. Indeed, following a mathematical induction argument, we show that, for any
2\leq k\leq K-1
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{N}{s\log d})\right)^{\frac{\beta}{2(1+\beta)}(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})}\Big(\frac{s\log d}{N}\Big)^{(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k})\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}},

(3.17)

with high probability. It can be verified from (
3.17
) that stopping early produces estimators with sub-optimal rates. This phenomenon occurs because, for
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
is not fast enough so that we have to choose a larger value of
b_{1}
to satisfy the event
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in (
3.11
). This remains the case, until
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
enters a fast convergence region

\Theta_{fast,\beta}=\Big\{\bm{\theta}:\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\Big(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\Big)^{1/(2\beta)}\sqrt{\log(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})}\Big\},

(3.18)

which is derived by matching the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
with the order of
\delta_{k}
up to a logarithmic factor (see the two conditions in (
3.11
)). Once
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\in\Theta_{fast,\beta}
, we only need one more iteration to achieve the parametric rate, mirroring the analysis of
K=2
in Theorem
2
.

Theorem 4

(Optimal rate for
0<\beta\leq 1
)
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold,
0<\beta\leq 1
and
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
. We set
N_{k}=N/K
for
1\leq k\leq K
,

\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}},

and for
2\leq k\leq K
,

\displaystyle b_{k-1}=

\displaystyle c_{3}\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{4\beta}[1-(\frac{1}{2\beta+1})^{k-1}]}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}[1-(\frac{1}{2\beta+1})^{k-1}]},

(3.19)

\displaystyle\delta_{k}=

\displaystyle c_{1}\left(\frac{b_{k-1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{k}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}\delta_{k}}},

(3.20)

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
. If

N\lesssim\Big(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}(Ks\log d)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}n^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},

(3.21)

and
Ks\log d=o(N)
hold, then with probability greater than
1-2Kd^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}.

(3.23)

For any
\beta\leq 1
, ignoring all the logarithmic factors, the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
in (
3.22
) reduces to
O_{p}((s/N)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}})
, which differs from the parametric rate
O_{p}(\sqrt{s/N})
in Theorems
2
and
3
. In addition, unlike the previous two cases (i)
\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
and (ii)
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, we have to run at least
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
iterations in Algorithm
1
to attain the optimal rate, where
K
has to grow with
N
, although, very slowly. To see the reason, we first apply a similar induction argument to show that, for
0<\beta\leq 1
and
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1})}\Big(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k})},

(3.24)

with high probability. Therefore, for any fixed
\beta\leq 1
and any fixed
k\geq 2
, the rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
in (
3.24
) is always slower than the bound in (
3.18
), and therefore the sequence of estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2},...,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k},...
can only approach the fast convergence region
\Theta_{fast,\beta}
but never enters it in finite
k
. Finally, we stop the algorithm at
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
steps when
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
is close enough to the fast convergence region. More precisely, we choose
K
such that the convergence rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K+1}
matches with that of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
.

Remark 2
.

Mallik et al. (2020)
studied a general M-estimation problem with multistage sampling procedures. In particular, they considered the following classification problem
d^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}\mathbb{E}[L_{01}(Y(X-d))]
, where
L_{01}(\cdot)
is the 0-1 loss. Under the assumption that
\eta(x)=\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x)
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
d^{*}
, they proposed an isotonic regression approach to estimate
\eta(x)
and then invert this function at
1/2
to estimate
d^{*}
. Using this approach, their first-stage estimator
\widehat{d}_{1}
with
N
i.i.d data sampled uniformly from the population has the rate
O_{p}(N^{-1/3})
. In the second stage, they sampled another
N
i.i.d data in a zoomed-in neighborhood of
\widehat{d}_{1}
and the resulting second-stage estimator
\widehat{d}_{2}
has the rate
O_{p}(N^{-(1+\gamma)/3})
for any
\gamma<1/3
, whose limiting distribution was also established. Their setting is similar to our
\beta=1
case. With
d,s
being fixed, the proof of Theorem
4
shows that our estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
has the rate
O_{p}((\log(\log N)/N)^{1/3})
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
has the rate
O_{p}((\log N)^{\frac{1}{6}}(\log(\log N)/N)^{\frac{4}{9}})
, which are comparable to the rates of
\widehat{d}_{1}
and
\widehat{d}_{2}
, respectively. In their paper, they did not pursue the theoretical results of the estimators beyond two stages, whereas our results confirm that the convergence rate can be further accelerated to the near-parametric rate
O_{p}((\log N)^{\frac{1}{4}}(\log(\log N)/N)^{\frac{1}{2}})
with
K=\lceil\log_{3}(\log N)\rceil
stages.

Remark 3
.

It is seen that the optimality of the proposed estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
critically depends on the choice of tuning parameters
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K},\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1},\{\delta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and the number of iterations
K
, which requires the knowledge of the smoothness parameter
\beta
and the sparsity parameter
s
. To overcome this limitation, we propose Lepski’s methods
(
Lepskii, 1991
)
to achieve adaptation to the unknown smoothness
\beta
and sparsity
s
respectively. The detailed theoretical guarantees are presented in Section
A.8
.

Finally, we briefly summarize our main conclusions:

(i)

\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
. The first step estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
already lies in the fast convergence region
\Theta_{fast,\beta}
; one more iteration yields the parametric rate.

(ii)

1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
. After
K-1=\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})\rceil
iterations, we have
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K-1}\in\Theta_{fast,\beta}
with high probability; the next step attains the parametric rate.

(iii)

0<\beta\leq 1
. There does not exist a finite
k
that places
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
inside
\Theta_{fast,\beta}
;
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
iterations are required to approach the fast convergence region.

3.3
Minimax Lower Bound

For clarity, we write
R_{P}(\bm{\theta})
for
R(\bm{\theta})
in (
1.2
), emphasizing that the expectation is taken under the joint distribution
P
of
(X,Y,\bm{Z})
. Similarly, we use
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
to denote the unique minimizer of
R_{P}(\bm{\theta})
. Let
\mathcal{P}(\beta,L,p_{\min},p_{\max})
denote the class of distributions which belong to the Hölder class
\mathcal{P}(\beta,L)
in Definition
3.1
and satisfy
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})<p_{\max}
and
\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}}f(x\mid\bm{z})\geq p_{\min}
, where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
are the support sets of
X
and
\bm{Z}
. We consider the following class of distributions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \big\{\,P\in&\mathcal{P}(\beta,L,p_{\min},p_{\max}):\|\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{0}\leq s,\;\|\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2}\leq C,\;\eqref{sparseeigen2}\ \text{holds},\\
&\rho_{-}\leq\lambda_{\min}\!\big(\nabla^{2}R_{P}(\bm{\theta}^{*}(P))\big)\leq\lambda_{\max}\!\big(\nabla^{2}R_{P}(\bm{\theta}^{*}(P))\big)\leq\rho_{+}\,\big\}\end{aligned}&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[15.0pt]
\begin{aligned} \big\{\,P\in&\mathcal{P}(\beta,L,p_{\min},p_{\max}):\|\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{0}\leq s,\;\|\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2}\leq C,\;\eqref{sparseeigen2}\ \text{holds},\\
&\rho_{-}\,\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2}^{1+\beta}\leq R_{P}(\bm{\theta})-R_{P}(\bm{\theta}^{*}(P))\leq\rho_{+}\,\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2}^{1+\beta},\\
&\qquad~~\text{for any }\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{2}\leq r_{n}\big\}\end{aligned}&\text{if }0<\beta\leq 1,\end{cases}

(3.25)

where we treat
L,p_{\min},p_{\max},C,M_{1},\rho_{-}
and
\rho_{+}
as positive constants, and
r_{n}
is a sequence converging to
0
. Thus
\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
encodes both the sparsity of
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
and the curvature of the population risk
R(\bm{\theta})
at
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
; see (
3.8
) for
\beta>1
and (
3.9
) for
0<\beta\leq 1
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, assume that
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})
are i.i.d. from the distribution
P
. We observe
O_{i}=(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})
if
R_{i}=1
and
O_{i}=(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
if
R_{i}=0
. Here, the assumed observed data mechanism is more flexible than that considered in Section
2
, as we can still observe the data
O_{i}=(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
even if the
i
th data point is not sampled. Let
\bar{H}_{i-1}=\{O_{1},...,O_{i-1}\}
denote the collection of the first
i-1
observed data, which can be viewed as the historical data prior to the
i
th sample. For simplicity, denote
\bar{H}_{i-1}=\emptyset
for
i=1
. In terms of the sampling procedure, we allow
R_{i}
to depend on
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
as well as the first
i-1
observed data
\bar{H}_{i-1}
. We denote
Q_{i}:=\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})
and
Q=(Q_{1},...,Q_{n})
. Based on the observed data
\{O_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, our goal is to estimate the unknown parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
. In this setting, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
is a measurable function of the observed data
(O_{1},...,O_{n})
, whose accuracy can be assessed by the following
l_{q}
risk

\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(O_{1},...,O_{n})-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{q},

where the expectation is taken under
(P,Q)
which determines the distribution of
(O_{1},...,O_{n})
. For any given sampling distribution
Q
, the minimax risk for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
is defined as

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s),Q):=\inf_{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(O_{1},...,O_{n})-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{q},

where the supremum is only for the distribution
P
in
\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
and the infimum is over all possible estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
based on the observed data
\{O_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

Since
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s),Q)
depends on the sampling distribution
Q
, to characterize the assumptions on
Q
, we define the following class. For any given distribution
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
and budget
N
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{N}(P):=\Big\{(Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{n})

\displaystyle:\forall 1\leq i\leq n,(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\perp_{(P,Q)}\bar{H}_{i-1},~~R_{i}\perp_{(P,Q)}Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1},

\displaystyle~~\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}\Big)\leq N,~~\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\right)\bm{v}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq CN\Big\},

(3.26)

where
C
is a positive constant. In (
3.26
),
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\perp_{(P,Q)}\bar{H}_{i-1}
and
R_{i}\perp_{(P,Q)}Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1}
formalize the assumptions on the data generating process for
R_{i}
. More precisely,
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\perp_{(P,Q)}\bar{H}_{i-1}
is valid, since we generate
(R_{1},...,R_{i-1})
without using the future data
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})
. In addition, the assumption
R_{i}\perp_{(P,Q)}Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1}
is satisfied by the measurement-constrained sampling, that is
Y_{i}
is not used to decide whether the
i
th data point is sampled or not. The assumption
\mathbb{E}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i})\leq N
is equivalent to
\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i})\leq N
, corresponding to our budget constraint. Similarly, the last assumption in (
3.26
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\right)\bm{v}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\lesssim N,

(3.27)

which controls the maximal sparse eigenvalues of
\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\right)
. When
Z
is univariate with bounded support, the condition (
3.27
) automatically holds, as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\left(R_{i}Z_{i}^{2}\right)\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}(R_{i})\leq N
, where the last inequality follows from the budget constraint. When the support of
Z
is unbounded, this assumption prevents sampling schemes that concentrate on extreme covariate values. They often correspond to high leverage points, that may indeed deteriorate the estimation accuracy.

Since the class
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(P)
depends on the given distribution
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)):=\cap_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)}\mathcal{Q}_{N}(P)
, as the set of sampling distributions that satisfy the conditions in (
3.26
) for all
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
.

Remark 4
.

We give some examples of sampling distributions in
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
.

•

Bounded-probability sampling. Consider an arbitrary sampling distribution
Q
, which satisfies the independence assumptions, the budget constraint
\mathbb{E}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i})\leq N
in (
3.26
) and more importantly a bounded probability assumption
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}Q_{i}\leq\frac{CN}{n}
for some constant
C>0
. To show
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(P)
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
, we note that for any
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s
,

\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\Big)\bm{v}\leq\frac{CN}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i})^{2}\leq CM_{1}N\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2},

where we use the assumption
Q_{i}\leq\frac{CN}{n}
in the first inequality, and the second inequality follows from
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
and the corresponding sparse eigenvalue assumption (
3.2
). Thus,
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
holds. The canonical example is the uniform sampling, where the data are sampled completely at random with
Q_{i}=\frac{N}{n}
. We note that, unlike the positivity assumption in the missing data literature, we do not impose any lower bound for
Q_{i}
, which allows
Q_{i}=0
in our scenario. Intuitively, the bounded probability assumption rules out extreme over-sampling of individual points relative to the uniform sampling.

•

Region-based sampling. Consider the following sampling distribution

Q_{i}=g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\ind\{f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})-g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\leq X_{i}\leq f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\},

(3.28)

where
f_{i}:(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})\rightarrow\mathbb{R},g_{1i},g_{2i}:\bar{H}_{i-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
g_{3i}:\bar{H}_{i-1}\rightarrow[0,1]
are user specified functions and
\mathbb{E}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i})\leq N
holds. Apparently, the independence assumptions in (
3.26
) are guaranteed by the data generating mechanism in (
3.28
). Moreover, in Section
A.10
we show that (
3.27
) holds for any
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
, which implies
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
. Recall that the proposed sampling mechanism in Section
2
can be written as follows, for any
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}
with
k>1
,

\displaystyle Q_{i}=c_{n,k}\ind\{-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\|_{2}^{2}}\leq X_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{H}_{i-1})^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}\leq b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\|_{2}^{2}}\},

where we write
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{H}_{i-1})
for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
to emphasize that it is a function of the historical data. Hence, our sampling method in Section
2
belongs to the class (
3.28
). Indeed, the class (
3.28
) covers the zoom-in approaches that oversample the data within a (small) region of
X
, complementing the bounded-probability sampling scheme. We also note that (
3.28
) can be generalized to the sampling distributions within multiple regions of
X
, such as
Q_{i}=g^{(k)}_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})
if
X_{i}\in S_{i}^{(k)}
and
Q_{i}=0
otherwise, for some
k>1
, where
S_{i}^{(k)}=[f^{(k)}_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})-g^{(k)}_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1}),f^{(k)}_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})+g^{(k)}_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})]
are non-overlapping intervals.

Finally, we define the
N
-budget minimax risk for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
as

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s),N):=\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))}\inf_{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)}\mathbb{E}_{P,Q}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(O_{1},...,O_{n})-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\|_{q}.

Theorem 5
.

Assume that
s(\frac{\log(d/s)}{N})^{1/2}=o(1)
. We have

\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))}\inf_{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)}\mathbb{P}_{P,Q}\left[\Big\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(O_{1},...,O_{n})-\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)\Big\|_{q}\geq cs^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2(\beta\wedge 1)}}\right]\geq c^{\prime}

for
q=1,2
, where
c,c^{\prime}
are positive constants.

Compared with the upper bounds in Section
3.2
, this theorem shows that our proposed estimator via the active subsampling algorithm is minimax rate optimal up to some logarithmic factors. By Markov inequality, we can also obtain the lower bound for the
N
-budget minimax risk

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s),N)\geq c^{\prime}cs^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2(\beta\wedge 1)}}.

As a final remark, the same lower bound in Theorem
5
holds if we restrict the infimum to estimators
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\{O_{i}\}_{i:R_{i}=1})
that use only the labeled data
\{O_{i}\}_{i:R_{i}=1}
. Indeed, this lower bound over
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(\{O_{i}\}_{i:R_{i}=1})
is tight, as it is attained by our estimators in Section
3.2
that only use the labeled data as well. Thus, the unlabeled samples
\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i:R_{i}=0}
offer no rate improvement for estimating
\bm{\theta}^{*}(P)
in our model.

4
Practical Considerations

In this section, we discuss several implementation issues in our Algorithm
1
.

First, we discuss the computational challenge for solving the optimization problem (
2.7
). Despite addressing the discontinuity of the 0-1 loss, the smoothed empirical risk function
R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
remains non-convex. Consequently, obtaining the global solution to (
2.7
) presents computational challenges. To overcome this issue, we focus on the path-following algorithm
(
Feng et al., 2022
)
. For any
1\leq k\leq K
, this algorithm computes approximate local solutions to (
2.7
) corresponding to a sequence of decreasing regularization parameters
\lambda
until the desired regularization parameter is reached. We set
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}
, where
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}
represents the final approximate local solution with the desired regularization parameter
\lambda_{k,tgt}
. By employing this path-following approach, we can efficiently compute the entire solution path for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
while preserving sparsity across the sequence. For
k\geq 2
, to speed up the computation of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
, we can use the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
obtained from the previous iteration as a warm start in the path-following approach. The detailed algorithm for solving (
2.7
) is provided in Appendix
A.3
.

Second, we consider how to choose
K
. Our results in Section
3.2
reveal that the choice of
K
may depend on the smoothness parameter
\beta
. In particular, when
\beta
is greater than
(1+\sqrt{3})/2\approx 1.37
, the two-step algorithm with
K=2
yields the estimator with the optimal rate. In practice, we recommend choosing
K=2
for the following reasons. First, in many applications, it is often reasonable to assume that the conditional density of
X
given
Y
and
\bm{Z}
is sufficiently smooth (e.g., twice differentiable), implying that
\beta>1.37
. Even if
1\leq\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, ignoring all logarithmic factors, the results (
3.17
) and (
3.24
) show that the slowest rate of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
in the
l_{2}
norm is
O_{p}((s/N)^{4/9})
, attained when
\beta=1
. In this case, the improvement of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
over
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
(which converges with rate
O_{p}((s/N)^{1/3})
) is much more substantial than the improvement of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{3}
(with rate
O_{p}((s/N)^{13/27})
) over
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
. Second, compared with
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
with
k>2
, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
depends on fewer tuning parameters, making it easier to implement and numerically more stable.

Finally, we discuss how to choose the tuning parameters
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
and
b_{1}
in our active subsampling algorithm with
K=2
. Empirically, the algorithm is insensitive to the choice of bandwidth parameters
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
. Thus, we set
\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}=1
in both simulation and real data analysis.
Indeed, following
Feng et al. (2022)
, we can easily modify our cross-validation algorithm to choose
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
,
b_{1}
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
simultaneously. However, such an approach tends to be computationally far more expensive and may require a very large
N
to obtain stable results. We do not pursue it here.

Instead, we apply the one standard error rule to choose
\lambda
in Algorithm
2
. Built on this algorithm, the data-driven two-step active subsampling with cross-validation is shown in Algorithm
3
. In this algorithm,
\widetilde{N}_{1}
,
\widetilde{N}_{cv}
and
\widetilde{N}_{2}
stand for the budget for obtaining
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
in step 1, the budget for selecting
b_{1}
in step 2, and the budget for obtaining the final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
in step 3, respectively. In general, we recommend choosing the budget
\widetilde{N}_{2}
in the final step relatively large, as
\widetilde{N}_{2}
plays the role of effective sample size for the final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
.

Algorithm 2

M
-fold Cross-Validation for
\lambda
with one standard error rule

Input:
Data
D=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\},\text{a grid for }\lambda
.

Parameters:

\delta

Randomly split
D
into
M
folds,
D_{1},...,D_{M}
with equal size.

For each
\lambda
in the grid, compute

\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}^{k}=\frac{1}{|D_{k}|}\sum_{a\in D_{k}}\gamma(Y_{a})L_{\delta}(Y_{a}(X_{a}-\bm{Z}^{T}_{a}\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{(-k)}_{\lambda})),

where
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}^{(-k)}_{\lambda}
is our estimator with the tuning parameters
\delta
and
\lambda
using the data excluding
D_{k}
.

Compute the cross-validation error
\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}^{k}
.

Define
\widehat{CV}_{\min}=\min_{\lambda}\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}
as the minimum cross-validation error over this grid, and
\bar{\lambda}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\lambda}\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}
. Define
\widehat{SE}_{\min}
as the standard error of
\{\widehat{CV}_{\bar{\lambda}}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{M}
over these M folds.

Find
\widehat{\lambda}_{CV}
via the following “one standard error rule”,

\widehat{\lambda}_{CV}=\max~\lambda\in\textrm{grid}~s.t.~\widehat{CV}_{\lambda}\leq\widehat{CV}_{\min}+\widehat{SE}_{\min}.

return

\widehat{\lambda}_{CV}
and
\widehat{CV}_{\widehat{\lambda}_{CV}}
.

Algorithm 3

Data-Driven Two-Step Active Subsampling with Cross-Validation

Input:

D=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\text{a grid $\Delta$ for }b
and
\widetilde{N}_{1},\widetilde{N}_{cv},\widetilde{N}_{2}
with
\widetilde{N}_{1}+\widetilde{N}_{cv}+\widetilde{N}_{2}=N
.

Parameters:

\delta_{1},\delta_{2}

Randomly split
D
into 3 batches with
|D_{1}|=\frac{\widetilde{N}_{1}}{N}n
,
|D_{cv}|=\frac{\widetilde{N}_{cv}}{N}n
and
|D_{2}|=\frac{\widetilde{N}_{2}}{N}n
.

Step 1: Obtain Initial Estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}

Draw data from
D_{1}
with probability
c_{n,1}=\frac{N}{n}
. Acquire the label
Y_{i}
for each sampled data and form the dataset
D_{1}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, where
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{1}
.

Apply the 5-fold cross-validation Algorithm
2
to
D_{1}^{*}
. Return the optimal parameter
\lambda_{1,opt}
.

Compute the initial estimator:
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{1,opt}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}
.

Step 2: Select Optimal
b_{1}

For each candidate
b\in\Delta
:

Define the active set:
S_{2}\leftarrow\left\{(X,\bm{Z}):-b\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}}}\leq b\right\}
.

Given
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{2}
, draw data
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
from
D_{cv}
with probability
\frac{N}{n|\Delta|\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)}
. Acquire the label
Y_{i}
for each sampled data and form
D_{cv,b}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, where
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{cv}
.

Apply the 5-fold cross-validation Algorithm
2
to
D_{cv,b}^{*}
. Return the optimal parameter
\lambda_{b,opt}
and the minimum CV error
\widehat{CV}_{b}
.

Compute
\widehat{b}_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{b\in\Delta}\widehat{CV}_{b}
that minimizes the cross-validation error.

Step 3: Obtain Final Estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}

Define the active set:
S_{2}\leftarrow\left\{(X,\bm{Z}):-\widehat{b}_{1}\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}}}\leq\widehat{b}_{1}\right\}
.

Given
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{2}
, draw data
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})
from
D_{2}
with probability
c_{n,2}=\frac{N}{n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)}
. Acquire the label
Y_{i}
for each sampled data and form
D_{2}^{*}=\{X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\}_{R_{i}=1}
, where
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{2}
.

Apply the 5-fold cross-validation Algorithm
2
to
D_{2}^{*}
. Return the optimal parameter
\lambda_{2,opt}
.

Compute the final estimator:
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{2}}_{\delta_{2}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{2,opt}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}
.

return

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}

5
Simulation Studies

We conduct simulations to evaluate the performance of our proposed method. We consider the following two classes of models, and for both models, it can be shown that the parameter
\bm{\theta}
coincides with the estimand in (
1.2
).

•

Binary response model:
We consider
Y=\operatorname{sign}(\widetilde{Y})
, where

\widetilde{Y}=X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}+\epsilon,

X\in\mathbb{R},\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\epsilon
is a random noise such that
\operatorname{Median}(\widetilde{Y}\mid X,\bm{Z})=X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}
. Logistic regression belongs to the class of binary response models by setting
\epsilon
to follow the logistic distribution independent with
(X,\bm{Z})
. We conduct simulations for both logistic regression and a more general case where we allow
\epsilon
to depend on
(X,\bm{Z})
.

•

Conditional mean model:
We consider
Y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

X=\mu Y+\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}+\epsilon,

where
\epsilon\perp Y,\bm{Z}
is a random noise and
\mu>0
is a constant.

Under each model, we simulate i.i.d. samples with
n=20000
and dimension
d=200
. We refer to the collection of these samples as the full dataset. We set sparsity
s=10
, and generate the nonzero elements of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
from
\text{Uniform}(1,2)
. We then normalize
\bm{\theta}^{*}
such that
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}=1
. For the logistic regression, we generate
X\sim N(0,1)
,
\bm{Z}\sim N_{d}(0,1)
. For the binary response model where
\epsilon
can depend on
(X,\bm{Z})
, we generate
X\sim N(0,1)
,
\bm{Z}\sim N_{d}(0,1)
, and
\epsilon\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}(1+2(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})^{2}))
with
\sigma=0.5
. We refer to this case as binary response model in the following discussion. For the conditional mean model, we generate
Y\sim\text{Uniform}\{-1,1\}
,
\bm{Z}\sim N_{d}(0,1)
,
\epsilon\sim N(0,(0.1)^{2})
and set
\mu=2
.

We fix the label budget at
N=2000
in all scenarios. For our proposed method, we implement the two-step active subsampling algorithm outlined in Algorithm
1
with
N_{1}=N/8
and
N_{2}=7N/8
. Empirically, we find that assigning a larger proportion of the label budget to the second step leads to a more stable final estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
. The numerical results for our algorithm with
N_{1}=N/5
and
N_{2}=4N/5
are quite similar and are deferred to the Appendix Section
A.11
. We compare the performance of our proposed method with
Feng et al. (2022)
, where
the path-following algorithm
is applied to a dataset with size
N=2000
uniformly sampled from the full dataset. We refer to this method as “passive PF”, while our proposed method is denoted as “two-step sampling with PF”. In the application of the path-following algorithm, we set the bandwidth parameter
\delta=1
and use the standard Gaussian density as the kernel function. The number of regularization stages is fixed at
T=20
, and we choose
\nu=1/4
,
\phi=(\lambda_{tgt}/\lambda_{0})^{1/T}
and
\eta=1
in the path-following Algorithm
4
. The tuning parameter
\lambda_{tgt}
is chosen by the 5-fold cross-validation via Algorithm
2
.

In our comparison, we also include two simple benchmarks “passive LR” and “two-step sampling with LR”. The former refers to the
\ell_{1}
-penalized logistic regression applied to the dataset with size
N=2000
uniformly sampled from the full dataset. For the “two-step sampling with LR”, we employ a similar two-step approach as in Algorithm
1
. However, instead of solving (
2.3
