) using the path-following algorithm, we estimate
\bm{\theta}
by the
\ell_{1}
-penalized logistic regression. Similar to our proposed method, the active set in second step has the same form as (
2.5
), which depends on the choice of
b_{1}
.

Figures
1
-
3
plot the statistical errors
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|
in
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
norms as the size of the active set
b_{1}
increases under three models. The smallest
b_{1}
value is chosen such that approximately 10% of the full dataset fall into the active set, while the largest
b_{1}
value corresponds to the case that the active set covers nearly 100% of the full dataset. The simulation is repeated 50 times.

Since the passive LR and passive PF methods are independent of
b_{1}
, their estimation errors correspond to two horizontal lines. In Figure
1
, the passive LR can be viewed as the benchmark estimator, since the data are generated under the logistic regression. We can see that applying the two-step sampling idea to logistic regression generally does not improve the performance of the passive LR. This may be due to the fact that the logistic regression is a regular model and the parameter estimation may not be improvable. However, our proposed two-step sampling with PF method can significantly improve upon the passive PF method, especially for small or moderate values of
b_{1}
. This is consistent with our theoretical results on the convergence rate of the estimators. Similar patterns hold under the conditional mean model in Figure
2
. In particular, our proposed method yields the smallest
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
estimation errors among all the competing methods, for small or moderate values of
b_{1}
. For the binary response model in Figure
3
, when
b_{1}
is small, our proposed method has larger errors than the passive PF method. However, as
b_{1}
keeps increasing, our proposed method outperforms the passive PF.

We also compare the four methods with respect to
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{\infty}
and prediction errors. The results exhibit similar patterns as
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}
and
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
; see Section
A.11
.

Since the optimal choice of
b_{1}
is data-dependent, we evaluate the fully data-driven procedure in Algorithm
3
. Table
1
compares our proposed method with the passive path-following method across the three models. The simulation is repeated 50 times. From the result in Table
1
we can see that our proposed method uniformly dominates the passive path-following method across all three models. For example, the
\ell_{1}
estimation error of our proposed method is around 45% smaller than the passive path-following method under the conditional mean model.

6
Real Data Analysis

In this section, we apply our proposed method to a dataset of hospitalized patient diagnosed with diabetes, obtained from the UCI Machine Learning Repository
(
Clore et al., 2014
)
. This dataset contains 101,766 hospital records collected over a decade (1999-2008) from 130 US hospitals. The data includes various attributes such as patient ID, race, gender, age, admission type, readmission status, length of hospital stay, medical specialty of the admitting physician, number of lab tests performed, Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) test results, diagnosis, and more. Among these attributes, the HbA1c test result is a key indicator of glucose control and is widely used to evaluate the quality of diabetes care
(
Baldwin et al., 2005
)
.
In an existing work
(
Strack et al., 2014
)
, researchers were interested in identifying important risk factors that lead to early readmission. They defined the readmission attribute as a binary outcome: “readmitted” if the patient was readmitted within 30 days of discharge, and “otherwise,” which includes both readmission after 30 days and no readmission.

In our analysis, we adopt the same definition of response variable
Y
as in
Strack et al. (2014)
. Specifically,
Y_{i}=1
if a patient was readmitted within 30 days of discharge and
Y_{i}=-1
otherwise. We chose the patient’s HbA1c test result as the primary measurement
X_{i}
, with
\bm{Z}_{i}
representing additional patient demographic statistics and clinical biomarkers. The primary goal of our analysis is to determine the optimal individualized threshold,
\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
, such that a patient’s early readmission can be predicted based on whether the HbA1c test result exceeds this threshold (
X_{i}\geq\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
) or falls below it (
X_{i}<\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
). This problem can be formulated as an estimation task of the form (
1.1
) or (
1.2
), with weights
\gamma(y)=1/\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
.

The original dataset includes multiple inpatient visits from the same patients, making the observations statistically dependent. To address this, as suggested by
Strack et al. (2014)
, we used only the first encounter per patient as the primary admission and determined whether they were readmitted within 30 days. Next, we removed redundant features and those with a high percentage of missing values. Following the discussion in
Strack et al. (2014)
, we also added pairwise interactions among features as new variables. After these preprocessing steps, the dataset contains 69,984 observations and
d=60
variables, excluding
X
. Furthermore, we observed significant imbalance in the dataset, with only 6,293 positive instances (
Y=1
) compared to 63,691 negative instances (
Y=-1
). To address this issue, we randomly selected 6,293 samples from the negative class to match the number of positive cases and then combined these with the positive instances. This resulted in a final dataset containing
n=12,586
observations.

Since the true value
\bm{\theta}^{*}
is unknown, we first applied the path-following algorithm to the full dataset (
n=12,586
) to derive an estimator, which is used as the benchmark or equivalently treated as
\bm{\theta}^{*}
when evaluating
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|
for different methods. Suppose that there is a budget constraint due to study design or administration cost, which prevents us from using the benchmark estimator. Our goal is to illustrate the efficacy of our active subsampling methods under the budget constraint. For our proposed method, we implement the data-driven two-step active subsampling algorithm outlined in Algorithm
3
with label budgets of
N=3000
, 4000, and 5000, respectively. We compare the performance of our method with “passive path-following,” where the budgeted data is uniformly sampled from the full dataset to derive the estimator of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
. The result is shown in Table
2
. It is seen that, as the label budget increases, the estimation errors for both methods decrease, with the two-step path-following method consistently outperforming the passive path-following method across all three settings. From a scientific point of view, our two-step path-following method identified a few important covariates in our model, including ‘time in hospital’ and ‘change of medications’. These findings are consistent with the previous work
(
Strack et al., 2014
)
, and seem to be clinically meaningful.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Further Examples

Example 1: Covariate-adjusted Youden index.

The Youden’s J statistic is a fundamental metric for evaluating the performance of a binary diagnostic test based on its Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve. Consider a continuous biomarker
X
and a binary disease status
Y
(
Y=1
if a patient is diseased and
Y=-1
if a patient is healthy). A common diagnostic rule classifies a patient as diseased if
X
exceeds a threshold
c
. Recent work, including
Xu et al. (2014)
, has shown that diagnostic accuracy can be improved by tailoring this threshold to patient-specific covariates
\bm{Z}
. This leads to a covariate-adjusted threshold of the form
c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z})
, where for simplicity we assume the threshold
c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z})=\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}
is linear in
\bm{Z}
. The covariate-adjusted Youden index is

J=\max_{\bm{\theta}}\{\text{sen}(c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z}))+\text{spe}(c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z}))-1\},

where
\text{sen}(c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z}))=\mathbb{P}(X\geq c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z})|Y=1)
and
\text{spe}(c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z}))=\mathbb{P}(X<c_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{Z})|Y=-1)
are the sensitivity and specificity of the test, respectively. Thus, estimating the covariate-adjusted Youden index
J
is equivalent to solving the problem (
1.1
).

Example 2: One-bit compressed sensing.
Classical compressed sensing aims to recover a high-dimensional signal
(\alpha^{*},\eta^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
from a small number of linear measurements
\widetilde{y}_{i}=x_{i}\alpha^{*}+\bm{z}_{i}^{T}\eta^{*}
, where
(x_{i},\bm{z}_{i})
is a measurement vector. In practice, these measurements
\widetilde{y}_{i}
are often quantized. One-bit compressed sensing represents an extreme case, where each measurement is quantized to a single bit based on the sign of
x_{i}\alpha^{*}+\bm{z}_{i}^{T}\eta^{*}

(
Boufounos and Baraniuk, 2008
)
. Assume that
\alpha^{*}>0
. The goal of one-bit compressed sensing is to recover the signal
\bm{\theta}^{*}
from the measurements

y_{i}=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(x_{i}-\bm{z}_{i}^{T}\bm{\theta}^{*}),

where we can only identify
\bm{\theta}^{*}=-\eta^{*}/\alpha^{*}
. We can easily verify that

\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\Big\{\pi\mathbb{P}\left(X<\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=1\right)+(1-\pi)\mathbb{P}\left(X>\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=-1\right)\Big\},

where
\pi=\mathbb{P}(Y=1)
. Therefore, the one-bit compressed sensing can be solved by minimizing a weighted version of (
1.1
).

Example 3: Semiparametric binary response model and maximum score estimator.

The binary response model plays a fundamental role in economic analysis, as many economic decisions and outcomes are inherently discrete, such as labor force participation, market entry, and credit default. Conventional parametric approaches, such as logistic regression, rely on stringent distributional assumptions for the response variable conditional on covariates. Once the distributional assumptions are violated, parameter estimation and inference may become unreliable. To mitigate potential model misspecification, the following well-known semiparametric binary response model is often preferred

Y=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(X\alpha^{*}+\bm{Z}^{T}\eta^{*}+\epsilon),

(A.1)

where
(X,\bm{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
are covariates and the random noise
\epsilon
is not required to be independent of
(X,\bm{Z})
. Similar to Example 2, we assume that
\alpha^{*}>0
. The model (
A.1
) can be equivalently written as
Y=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(X-\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\epsilon)
, where only
\bm{\theta}^{*}=-\eta^{*}/\alpha^{*}
is identifiable. Under the assumption that the conditional median of
\epsilon
given
X
and
\bm{Z}
is 0, we can similarly verify that

\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\Big\{\pi\mathbb{P}\left(X<\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=1\right)+(1-\pi)\mathbb{P}\left(X>\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=-1\right)\Big\},

where
\pi=\mathbb{P}(Y=1)
. As a seminal work,
Manski (1975)
proposed the maximum score estimator for
\bm{\theta}^{*}
by minimizing a weighted empirical version of (
1.1
). Thus, our framework includes the semiparametric binary response model as a special case.

Example 4: Optimal individualized treatment rules and policy learning.
Many clinical studies have demonstrated that patient responses to the same treatment can be highly heterogeneous. Motivated by this heterogeneity, determining the optimal treatment strategy for each individual has become a central problem in personalized medicine. Similarly, decision making problems are ubiquitous in economics and marketing. For instance, a firm must decide which customers will receive targeted offers. The government may need to decide which groups of participants to prioritize in a welfare-to-work program. In the statistics and economics literature, extensive research has focused on developing methods to estimate optimal individualized treatment rules (ITRs) or optimal policy using high-dimensional covariates.

Let
R
denote a continuous outcome variable coded such that higher values indicate better conditions,
(X,\bm{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
represent baseline features, and
A\in\{-1,1\}
denote the assigned treatment. An ITR,
D(\cdot)
, is a mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
to
\{-1,1\}
, prescribing treatment
D(X,\bm{Z})
for a patient with covariates
(X,\bm{Z})
.
Zhao et al. (2012)
showed that the optimal ITR
D^{*}
minimizes the weighted misclassification risk

\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{R}{\pi(A|X,\bm{Z})}\mathds{1}(A\neq D(X,\bm{Z}))\Big],

where
\pi(a|X,\bm{Z})=\mathbb{P}(A=a|X,\bm{Z})
is the propensity score.

In practice, simple ITRs, such as linear decision rules, are often preferred for interpretability and clinical applicability. When some prior domain knowledge can identify the direction of association for certain baseline variables (e.g.,
X
has a positive effect), the linear ITR can be expressed as
D_{\bm{\theta}}(X,\bm{Z})=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})
. Consequently, estimating the optimal ITR reduces to solving

\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\Big\{\gamma_{1}(X,\bm{Z})\mathbb{P}\left(X<\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid A=1\right)+\gamma_{-1}(X,\bm{Z})\mathbb{P}\left(X>\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid A=-1\right)\Big\},

where
\gamma_{a}(X,\bm{Z})=\mathbb{E}(R\mid A=a,X,\bm{Z})\mathbb{P}(A=a)/\pi(a\mid X,\bm{Z})
for
a\in\{-1,1\}
. When the weight function
\gamma_{a}(X,\bm{Z})
is known or can be consistently estimated, our framework can be used to estimate the optimal ITR.

Example 5: High-dimensional linear classifiers.
The framework introduced in Example 4 can be further extended to a general binary classification setting. In particular, let
(X,\bm{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
denote the feature vector and
Y\in\{-1,1\}
be the binary label. In the high-dimensional setting, where
d
is much larger than the sample size, to avoid the curse of dimensionality, a linear classifier
D_{\bm{\theta}}(X,\bm{Z})=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})
is often desirable, where we also assume that
X
has a positive effect. In the model-agnostic setting, the optimal linear classifier
D_{\bm{\theta}^{*}}(X,\bm{Z})
can be defined as the minimizer of a weighted misclassification risk

\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\Big\{\gamma_{1}(X,\bm{Z})\mathbb{P}\left(X<\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=1\right)+\gamma_{-1}(X,\bm{Z})\mathbb{P}\left(X>\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\theta}\mid Y=-1\right)\Big\},

where
\gamma_{1}(X,\bm{Z})
and
\gamma_{-1}(X,\bm{Z})
are nonnegative weight functions, which are known or can be estimated from external information. Hence, our framework naturally extends to the estimation of high-dimensional linear classifiers, providing a flexible approach for classification problems under minimal distributional assumptions.

A.2
Comments on Estimating
\mathbb{P}\left((X,Z)\in S_{k}\right)

When
p_{k}:=\mathbb{P}\left((X,Z)\in S_{k}\right)
is unknown, we can use the following sample splitting approach to modify our method. We randomly divide the dataset
D
into
2K-1
batches
D_{1},D_{20},D_{21},\cdots,D_{K0},D_{K1}
. In the first iteration, we uniformly sample data from
D_{1}
with probability
0<c_{n,1}<1
,

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1)=c_{n,1}=\frac{N_{1}K}{n},

where
N_{1}
is the expected number of data points sampled in the first iteration. We compute the estimator

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{1}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}.

Starting from the second iteration (i.e.,
k\geq 2
), we use the dataset
D_{k0}
to estimate
p_{k}=\mathbb{P}\left((X,Z)\in S_{k}\right)
,

\widehat{p}_{k}=\frac{1}{|D_{k0}|}\sum_{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k0}}\ind\left\{\left(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\right)\in S_{k}\right\}.

Then, given
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k1}
,
\widehat{p}_{k}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we generate
R_{i}
from a Bernoulli distribution with

\mathbb{P}\left(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\widehat{p}_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)=\widehat{c}_{n,k}\cdot\ind\left\{\left(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\right)\in S_{k}\right\},

where
\widehat{c}_{n,k}=N_{k}K/\left(n\widehat{p}_{k}\right)
. Finally, we compute the estimator from the dataset
D_{k1}
,

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{k1}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{k}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\}.

By splitting the data into
2K-1
batches
D_{1},D_{20},D_{21},\cdots,D_{K0},D_{K1}
, we can now easily establish the concentration inequality for
\widehat{p}_{k}
. In this case, conditioning on
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, the terms
\ind\left\{\left(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\right)\in S_{k}\right\}
are independent, and thus by Bernstein inequality,

\mathbb{P}(|\widehat{p}_{k}-{p}_{k}|\geq t|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{|D_{k0}|t^{2}}{2p_{k}(1-p_{k})+\frac{2}{3}t}\right).

Since the right side doesn’t contain
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}(|\widehat{p}_{k}-{p}_{k}|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{|D_{k0}|t^{2}}{2p_{k}(1-p_{k})+\frac{2}{3}t}\right).

Define the event
E=\{|\widehat{p}_{k}-p_{k}|\leq u\}
, and thus
P(E^{c})\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{|D_{k0}|u^{2}}{2p_{k}(1-p_{k})+\frac{2}{3}u}\right)
. On the event
E
, we can bound
\widehat{c}_{n,k}=\frac{N_{k}K}{n\widehat{p}_{k}}
by
\frac{c_{n,k}}{1+u/p_{k}}\leq\widehat{c}_{n,k}\leq\frac{c_{n,k}}{1-u/p_{k}}
.
Finally, we can bound the unconditional probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>t\mid E)\mathbb{P}(E)+\mathbb{P}(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>t\mid E^{c})\mathbb{P}(E^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>t,E)+\mathbb{P}(E^{c})

\displaystyle\leq 2d\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}t^{2}n/K}{C_{2}\frac{c_{n,k}}{(1-u/p_{k})\delta_{k}}+\frac{t}{3}C_{1}M_{n}K_{\max}/\delta_{k}}\right)+2\exp\left(-\frac{|D_{k0}|u^{2}}{2p_{k}(1-p_{k})+\frac{2}{3}u}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}t^{2}n/K}{2C_{2}\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}+\frac{t}{3}C_{1}M_{n}K_{\max}/\delta_{k}}\right)+2\exp\left(-\frac{|D_{k0}|p_{k}^{2}/4}{2p_{k}(1-p_{k})+\frac{1}{3}p_{k}}\right)

by taking
u=p_{k}/2
. By the proof of Theorem
2
, we have shown that
p_{k}\asymp b_{k}
. Then, with
t=C\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
,

\mathbb{P}(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>C\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}})\leq 2d^{-1}+2d^{-1},

where we assume
b_{k}\geq C^{\prime}\frac{\log d}{|D_{k0}|}
in order to bound the second term. Since we have a very large unlabeled dataset, we can take
|D_{k0}|=c^{\prime}n/K
for some constant
0<c^{\prime}<1
. Then, the same conclusion as in Proposition
A.1
holds, under a very mild assumption
b_{k}\gtrsim\frac{K\log d}{n}
. By carefully examining the rest of the proof, we can conclude that the convergence rate of the proposed estimator remains the same.

A.3
Path-following Algorithm

We employ the path-following algorithm introduced in
Feng et al. (2022)
to solve the optimization problem
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}:=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}\{R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{k}\|\bm{\theta}\|\}
for each iteration
k
.
The idea is to compute approximate local solutions corresponding to a sequence of decreasing regularization parameters
\lambda
, until the target regularization parameter is reached. To be specific, we firstly choose a sequence of
\lambda_{k,0}>\lambda_{k,1}>\ldots>\lambda_{k,T}=\lambda_{k,tgt}
, where
\lambda_{k,t}=\phi^{t}\lambda_{k,0},t=0,1,\ldots
for some constant
\phi\in(0,1)
and
\lambda_{k,tgt}
is the target regularization parameter to be specified later. Let
T
denote the total number of the path-following
stages and we set
T=\frac{\log\left(\lambda_{k,tgt}/\lambda_{k,0}\right)}{\log\phi}
.
At each stage
t=1,\cdots,T
, the goal is to approximately compute the exact local solution
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}
corresponding to
\lambda_{k,t}
,

\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}=\underset{\bm{\theta}}{\operatorname{argmin}}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda_{k,t}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}.

(A.2)

To this end, we apply the proximal-gradient method to iteratively approximate
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}
by minimizing a sequence of quadratic approximations of
R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})
over a convex constraint set
\Omega
:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j+1}

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{\theta}\in\Omega}{\operatorname{argmin}}\left\{R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}\right)+\left\langle\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}\right),\left(\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}\right)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2\eta}\left\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{k,t}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\right\}

(A.3)

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{\theta}\in\Omega}{\operatorname{argmin}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\eta}\left\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}+\eta\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}\left(\bm{\theta}_{k,t}^{j}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{k,t}\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{k,t}\eta}\left(\bm{\theta}^{j}_{k},\Omega\right),

where
\eta
is the step size to be specified later.
The proximal-gradient algorithm is described in Algorithm
5
.

In the algorithm, we use the stopping criteria
w_{\lambda}(\bm{\theta})
defined as

\omega_{\lambda}(\bm{\theta})=\min_{\bm{\xi}\in\partial\|\bm{\theta}\|_{1}}\max_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\left\{\frac{\left(\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right)^{T}}{\left\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right\|_{1}}\left(\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})+\lambda\bm{\xi}\right)\right\}.

At stage
t
, the proximal-gradient algorithm returns an approximate solution

\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}
with precision
\epsilon_{k,t}=\nu\lambda_{k,t},\nu\in(0,1)
corresponding to
\lambda_{k,t}
. Then we use
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}
as a warm start for stage
t+1
and repeat this process. At the final stage, we would compute the
approximate solution
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,T}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}

corresponding to
\lambda_{k,tgt}
using a high precision
\epsilon_{k,tgt}
. The detail of the path-following algorithm is described in Algorithm
4
.

We note that at iteration
k
, we can use the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
obtained from iteration
k-1
as the initial value for
\bm{\theta}
in Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

\bm{\theta}\leftarrow
Path-Following
\left(\lambda_{k,0},\lambda_{k,tgt},\nu,T,\epsilon_{k,tgt},\Omega\right)

input:

\lambda_{k,tgt}>0,\nu>0,\phi>0,\epsilon_{k,tgt}>0,\Omega

parameter:

\eta>0

initialize:

\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,0}\leftarrow\mathbf{0},\lambda_{k,0}\leftarrow\left\|\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\mathbf{0})\right\|_{\infty},T\leftarrow\frac{\log\left(\lambda_{k,tgt}/\lambda_{k,0}\right)}{\log\phi}

for

t=1,\ldots,T-1

do

\lambda_{k,t}\leftarrow\phi^{t}\lambda_{k,0}\quad\epsilon_{k,t}\leftarrow\nu\lambda_{k,t}\quad\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t}\leftarrow\text{ Proximal-Gradient }\left(\lambda_{t},\epsilon_{k,t},\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,t-1},\Omega\right)

end

for

\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,T}\leftarrow
Proximal-Gradient
\left(\lambda_{k,tgt},\epsilon_{k,tgt},\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,T-1},\Omega\right)

return

\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,T}

Algorithm 5

\bm{\theta}\leftarrow
Proximal-Gradient
\left(\lambda,\epsilon,\bm{\theta}^{0},\Omega\right)

input:

\lambda>0,\epsilon>0,\bm{\theta}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\Omega

parameter:

\eta>0

initialize:

j\leftarrow 0

while

w_{\lambda}\left(\bm{\theta}^{j}\right)>\epsilon

do

j\leftarrow j+1\quad\bm{\theta}^{j+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\lambda\eta}\left(\bm{\theta}^{j},\Omega\right)

end

while

return

\bm{\theta}^{j+1}

A.4
Preliminary Results

Recall that we consider the case
\gamma(y)=1/\mathbb{P}(Y=y)
and for simplicity denote
\|\widehat{\bm{\omega}}_{k}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\|_{2}^{2}}
in the rest of the proof.

We also allow the kernel with unbounded support which satisfies the following tail condition. Assume that there exits a sequence
C_{N}>0
that may depend on
N
such that

\int_{C_{N}/2}^{\infty}|K(t)|dt\leq C\delta_{k}^{\beta},

(A.4)

holds for any
2\leq k\leq K
, where
C
is a constant that does not depend on
k
and
\delta_{k}
is the bandwidth parameter in the
k
th iteration.

For example, the Gaussian kernel satisfies

\int_{C_{N}/2}^{\infty}|K(t)|dt\leq\frac{C}{C_{N}}e^{-C_{N}^{2}/8}
.
Note that in Theorem
6
, we choose
\delta_{k}\asymp\left(\frac{C_{N}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)}
, for
2\leq k\leq K
. In this case, (
A.4
) requires
\frac{e^{-C_{N}^{2}/8}}{C_{N}}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{C_{N}Ks\log d}{N}}
, which holds with
C_{N}\asymp\sqrt{\log N}
. For the kernel with bounded support, we can simply set
C_{N}=O(1)
in the rest of the proof.

Lemma A.1
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
(i) and (ii),
3.3
and
3.4
, we have for any
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\in D_{k}
,
2\leq k\leq K
, and for all
j=1\cdots,d
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\gamma(Y_{i})\frac{Y_{i}Z_{ij}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{Y_{i}\left(X_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}_{i}\right)}{\delta_{k}}\right)R_{i}\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]\leq C\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}},

for some constant
C>0
.

Proof.

For simplicity we omit the subscript
i
in the proof.
Note that since
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
is independent of
(X,\bm{Z},Y)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\gamma(Y)\frac{YZ_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{Y\left(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}\right)}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\gamma(Y)\frac{YZ_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{Y\left(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}\right)}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y\right]\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=-1\right].

We bound the first term here and the second term follows similarly. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1,X,\bm{Z}\right]\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right].

Recall that

S_{k}:=\left\{(X,\bm{Z}):-b_{k-1}\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\|\widehat{\bm{\omega}}_{k-1}\|_{2}}\leq b_{k-1}\right\},

and

\mathbb{P}(R=1\mid X,\bm{Z},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})=c_{n,k}\cdot\ind\{(X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\},

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1,X,\bm{Z}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)\right)^{2}\cdot\ind\{(X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\},

where we use the fact that
R\perp Y\mid(X,\bm{Z},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)\right)^{2}\cdot\ind\{(X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{\delta_{k}^{2}}\int_{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}^{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}K^{2}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)f(x\mid\bm{z},Y=1)dxf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}\int_{\bm{z}}\int_{(-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z})/\delta_{k}}^{(b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z})/\delta_{k}}z_{j}^{2}K^{2}(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}.

Since
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})<p_{\max}<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z})/\delta_{k}}^{(b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z})/\delta_{k}}K^{2}(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)du\leq p_{\max}\int K^{2}(u)du.

Note that
\mathbb{E}(Z_{j}^{2}|Y=1)\leq M_{1}<\infty
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{Z_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\right)^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},Y=1\right]\leq\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}M_{1}p_{\max}\int K^{2}(u)du,

hence we finish the proof.
∎

Proposition A.1
.

Denote
\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\mathbb{E}\left(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)
for
2\leq k\leq K
.
Under Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
(i) and (ii),
3.3
and
3.4
, with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
, we have

\|\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}},

where
C_{1}
is a constant independent of
n
,
d
and
k
.

Proof.

Denote
T^{k}=\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
. By definition

\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}=\left\|\frac{K}{n}\sum_{(x_{i},\bm{z}_{i})\in D_{k}}\left(\gamma(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z}_{i}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{y_{i}(x_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i})}{\delta_{k}})R_{i}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(Y)\frac{Y\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{Y(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z})}{\delta_{k}})R\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]\right\|_{\infty}.

Note that for some constant
C_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle|T^{k}_{ij}|=

\displaystyle\left|\gamma(y_{i})\frac{y_{i}\bm{z}_{ij}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{y_{i}(x_{i}-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}_{i})}{\delta_{k}})R_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(Y)\frac{YZ_{j}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{Y\left(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}\right)}{\delta_{k}}\right)R\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\frac{M_{n}K_{\max}}{\delta_{k}},

and by Lemma
A.1
we know that for some constant
C_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{E}((T_{ij}^{k})^{2}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\leq C_{2}\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}.

Then by Bernstein inequality we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|T^{k}\|_{\infty}>t\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}\left(|T_{j}^{k}|>t\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2d\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}t^{2}n/K}{C_{2}\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}+\frac{t}{3}C_{1}M_{n}K_{\max}/\delta_{k}}\right).

Since the right side doesn’t contain
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\left(\|\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{\infty}>t\right)\leq 2d\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}t^{2}n/K}{C_{2}\frac{c_{n,k}}{\delta_{k}}+\frac{t}{3}C_{1}M_{n}K_{\max}/\delta_{k}}\right).

Then note that
M_{n}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}n\delta_{k}}{K\log d}}
and take
t=C_{3}\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}
for some constant
C_{3}
we finish the proof.‘
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Recall that
\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\bm{\theta}}R(\bm{\theta})
, where
R(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\left(\gamma(Y)L_{01}(Y(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}))\right)
, and
\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\mathbb{E}\left(\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)
for
2\leq k\leq K
. Consider the following two cases:

(i)

Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
(i) and (ii),
3.3
and
3.4
hold, and

b_{k-1}\geq C_{N}\delta_{k}\text{\ and\ }b_{k-1}\geq 2\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}.

(A.5)

(ii)

Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
(i), (ii) and (iii),
3.3
and
3.4
hold, and

b_{k-1}\geq C_{N}\delta_{k},~~\textrm{and}~~b_{k-1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\frac{N}{Ks\log d}},

(A.6)

where
C_{N}>0
is defined in (
A.4
) and there exists a large constant
\zeta
such that
(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{\zeta}\lesssim\delta_{k}
with
\delta_{k}=o(1)
.

If either (i) or (ii) holds, we have for any
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}
,

\left|\bm{v}^{T}\left(\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\right)\right|\leq C_{2}c_{n,k}\delta_{k}^{\beta}\|\bm{v}\|_{2},

where
s^{\prime}
is defined in Assumption
3.2
and
C_{2}
is a constant independent of
n
,
d
and
k
.

Proof.

By definition we have

\displaystyle R(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\left(\ind(X<\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})\mid Y=1\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\ind(X>\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})\mid Y=-1\right),

and

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)f(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=-1\right)f(\bm{z}\mid Y=-1)d\bm{z}.

Note that

\displaystyle\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\gamma(Y)\frac{Y\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{Y(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z})}{\delta_{k}})R\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=-1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right),

hence

\displaystyle\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)-c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)f(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=-1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)+c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=-1\right)f(\bm{z}\mid Y=-1)d\bm{z}.

We will bound the first term and the second term follows similarly. Denote

S_{u}=\{u:\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\leq u\leq\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\}.

We have

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\int_{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}^{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}K\left(\frac{x-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)f(x\mid\bm{z},Y=1)dxf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle~~~-c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}}K(\frac{X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}}{\delta_{k}})R\mid Y=1,\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right)-c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{z}f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)f(\bm{z},Y=1)d\bm{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\underbrace{\int K(u)\left(f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)-f(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)\right)du}_{(A)}f(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}.

(A.7)

Now we look at the first term when
\beta>1
. Since
f(x\mid\bm{z},y)
is
l
times differentiable, by Taylor expansion we have

\displaystyle f\left(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)-f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\frac{f^{(i)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)}{i!}(u\delta_{k})^{i}+\frac{(u\delta_{k})^{l}}{l!}f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta_{k}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)

for some
\tau\in[0,1]
where
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
.
By the definition of kernel of order
l
, we obtain

\displaystyle(A)=\int K(u)\frac{(u\delta_{k})^{l}}{l!}\left(f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta_{k}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)-f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)\right)du,

hence for any
\bm{v}
with
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\left|c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int K(u)\left(f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)-f(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)\right)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|c_{n,k}\int K(u)\frac{(u\delta_{k})^{l}}{l!}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\left(f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta_{k}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)-f^{(l)}\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)\right)f(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}du\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int|K(u)|\frac{|u\delta_{k}|^{l}}{l!}L\|\bm{v}\|_{2}|u\delta_{k}|^{\beta-l}du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{n,k}L\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\int|K(u)|\frac{|u\delta_{k}|^{\beta}}{l!}du.

When
0<\beta\leq 1
, by definition (
3.1
), we have

|f\left(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)-f\left(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1\right)|\leq L\delta_{k}^{\beta}|u|^{\beta},

hence for any
\bm{v}
with
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\left|c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int K(u)\left(f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)-f(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)\right)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\int|K(u)|\int_{\bm{z}}|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}|L\delta_{k}^{\beta}|u|^{\beta}f(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{n,k}L\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\delta_{k}^{\beta}\int|K(u)||u|^{\beta}du.

For the second term on the right hand side of (
A.7
), we first show the result under (
A.5
), i.e.,

b_{k-1}\geq C_{N}\delta_{k}\text{\ and\ }b_{k-1}\geq 2\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}
. Then
we have

\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\geq\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}},

and

\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\leq\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}}.

Therefore, by (
A.4
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{S_{u}^{c}}|K(u)|du=

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du+\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du+\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}}}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{C_{N}/2}^{\infty}|K(u)|du=O(\delta_{k}^{\beta}),

where the last inequality follows that
\|\widehat{\bm{\omega}}_{k-1}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq 1
, hence

\displaystyle\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}M_{n}}{\delta_{k}}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-b_{k-1}/2}{\delta_{k}}\geq\frac{b_{k-1}}{2\delta_{k}}\geq C_{N}/2.

Finally, since
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})<p_{\max}
, and
\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y\right)\bm{v}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq L^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|c_{n,k}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c_{n,k}p_{\max}|\mathbb{E}(|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}|\mid Y=1)|\delta_{k}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c_{n,k}p_{\max}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}((\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{2}\mid Y=1)}\delta_{k}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c_{n,k}p_{\max}\|\bm{v}\|_{2}L\delta_{k}^{\beta}.

Combining the bound for the two terms in (
A.7
) we finish the proof.

Now we show the result also holds under (
A.6
), i.e.,

b_{k-1}\geq C_{N}\delta_{k},~~\textrm{and}~~b_{k-1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\frac{N}{Ks\log d}}
. For the second term on the right hand side of (
A.7
), we only need to check that

\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}=O(\delta_{k}^{\beta}).

Since
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x\mid y,\bm{z})<p_{\max}
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)f(u\delta_{k}+\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},Y=1)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p_{\max}\int_{\bm{z}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}|\leq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}}_{\text{A}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}|>C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}}\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int_{S_{u}^{c}}K(u)duf(\bm{z}\mid Y=1)d\bm{z}}_{\text{B}}.

For the term
A
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{S_{u}^{c}}|K(u)|du=

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du+\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}/2}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du+\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}/2}{\delta_{k}}}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int_{\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}/2}{\delta_{k}}}^{\infty}|K(u)|du

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\int_{C_{N}/2}^{\infty}|K(u)|du=O(\delta_{k}^{\beta}),

where the last inequality follows that
\|\widehat{\bm{\omega}}_{k-1}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq 1
, (
A.4
) and

\displaystyle\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}/2}{\delta_{k}}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{b_{k-1}\|\widehat{\bm{w}}_{k-1}\|_{2}-b_{k-1}/2}{\delta_{k}}\geq\frac{b_{k-1}}{2\delta_{k}}\geq C_{N}/2.

For the term
B
, since
(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}\mid Y=1
is sub-Gaussian with sub-Gaussian norm that scales with
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|>C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}\mid Y=1\right)\leq 2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{C^{\prime}},

hence we can choose
C
sufficiently large such that
C^{\prime}/2\geq\zeta\beta
and thus
\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{C^{\prime}/2}\lesssim\delta_{k}^{\beta}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle B\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}\ind\{|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|>C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}\}\mid Y=1\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathbb{E}((\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z})^{2}\mid Y=1)}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}\left(|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|>C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}\mid Y=1\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{C^{\prime}/2}\lesssim\|\bm{v}\|_{2}\delta_{k}^{\beta}.

Combining the bound for the two terms in (
A.7
) we finish the proof.

∎

A.5
Proof of the Main Results

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Note that our estimator is defined as
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}:=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}
, where
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k,tgt}
represents the approximate local solution from the path-following algorithm. Therefore, by Theorem
9
, we have that with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,k}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}},

(A.8)

and

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,k}}\right)^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}}.

(A.9)

When
k=1
, we have

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1)=c_{n,1},\ \mathbb{P}(R_{i}=0)=1-c_{n,1},

hence
N_{1}=nc_{n,1}/K
. Plugging this back to (
A.8
) and (
A.9
) we get the result for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
. For
2\leq k\leq K
, recall that

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})=c_{n,k}\cdot\ind\{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{k}\}.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(R_{i})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[R_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[c_{n,k}\cdot\ind\{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in S_{k}\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{n,k}\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right),

hence
N_{k}=n\mathbb{E}(R_{i})/K=nc_{n,k}\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)/K
. Plugging this back to (
A.8
) and (
A.9
) we finish the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

First, let’s consider the case when
k=1
. For each
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{1}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}=1)=c_{n,1},\ \mathbb{P}(R_{i}=0)=1-c_{n,1},

and
N/K=N_{1}=nc_{n,1}/K
.
According to Theorem
1
, by selecting

\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,1}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}=c_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}

and
\lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,1}K\log d}{n\delta_{1}}}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}}
, with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
, we obtain

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)},~~~\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}.

(A.10)

Next, we will establish the bound for
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
. The result for
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}
follows similarly.
According to Assumption
3.3
, we have
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x\mid\bm{z})<p_{\max}<\infty
. Recall that

S_{k}:=\left\{(X,\bm{Z}):-b_{k-1}\leq\frac{X-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}}\leq b_{k-1}\right\}.

Let us start from
k=2
.
Since
\bm{Z}|Y=y
is sub-Gaussian with a bounded sub-Gaussian norm and independent of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}
, we have
(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|Y=y
is also sub-Gaussian, with a sub-Gaussian norm that scales with
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
, hence

\mathbb{P}\left(|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|>c\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}\right)\leq 2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{c^{\prime}},

where
c^{\prime}
is a sufficiently large constant. Consider the following set

\mathcal{E}=\Big\{(\bm{\theta},\bm{Z}):\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{2}\leq 2C,|({\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\bm{Z}|\leq c\|{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}\Big\},

where
C
is the constant defined in Assumption
3.1
. Note that
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}\leq C
with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
. Since
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}\leq\|\bm{\theta}^{*}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}+\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
, the event
(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E}
holds with probability greater than
1-2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{c^{\prime}}-2d^{-1}
.
So we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z}=\bm{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}^{b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}f(x\mid\bm{z})dx

\displaystyle\leq 2b_{k-1}p_{\max}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}.

As a result, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z}\right)\ind\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E}\}\right)

\displaystyle~~~~+\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z}\right)\ind\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\notin\mathcal{E}\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2b_{k-1}p_{\max}\sqrt{1+4C^{2}}+\mathbb{P}((\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\notin\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle\leq 2b_{k-1}p_{\max}\sqrt{1+4C^{2}}+2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{c^{\prime}}+2d^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim b_{k-1},

(A.11)

where
b_{k-1}=c_{3}(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/(2\beta)}
and the last step follows from the fact that
d\gg N
and
c^{\prime}
is sufficiently large. In addition, it can be shown that

\displaystyle-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}=

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\left(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\right)^{T}\bm{z}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}-C_{1}b_{k-1},

if the events
(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E}
and

\displaystyle c\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\Big)}\leq b_{k-1}/2,

hold. By (
A.10
) and the choice of
b_{k-1}
, the two events hold with probability greater than
1-2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{c^{\prime}}-2d^{-1}
. By a similar proof, we can show that

b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}\leq\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+C_{2}b_{k-1}.

Therefore, with probability greater than
1-2\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{c^{\prime}}-2d^{-1}
, the event

\mathcal{A}_{k}=\Big\{\Big[-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z},b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}\Big]\subset B(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z},\epsilon)\Big\},

holds, where
\epsilon=C_{3}b_{k-1}
for some constant
C_{3}
large enough. Following the similar derivations in (
A.11
), by Assumption
3.3
, we can show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbb{P}\Big((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\mid\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z}\Big)\ind\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{k}\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big\{\ind\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{k}\}\int_{-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}^{b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}f(x\mid\bm{z})dx\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\Big\{\ind\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{k},\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{G}\}\int_{-b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}^{b_{k-1}\sqrt{1+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\|_{2}^{2}}+\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}^{T}\bm{z}}f(x\mid\bm{z})dx\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq 2b_{k-1}p_{\min}\mathbb{P}\{(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1},\bm{Z})\in\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}_{k},\bm{Z}\in\mathcal{G}\}\geq Cb_{k-1},

(A.12)

for some constant
C
. Combining (
A.11
) with (
A.12
), we have
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)\asymp b_{k-1}
.

To apply Theorem
1
, we need to verify that
b_{k-1}
satisfies

b_{k-1}\geq C\delta_{k}~~\text{\ and \ }~~b_{k-1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}.

(A.13)

Given
b_{k-1}=c_{3}(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/(2\beta)}
and from Theorem
1

\delta_{k}\asymp\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,k}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{s\log d~~\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)}{N_{k}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{b_{k-1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},

(A.14)

where it follows from
N_{k}=n\mathbb{E}(R_{i})/K=nc_{n,k}\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)/K
, we can verify that
b_{k-1}\geq C\delta_{k}
holds. In addition, by (
A.10
),
Ks\log d=o(N)
and for any fixed
\beta>\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
(which implies
\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}>\frac{1}{2\beta}
), we conclude that
b_{k-1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}
holds with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
. Thus, applying Theorem
1
with
\delta_{k}
in (
A.14
) and
\lambda_{k}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}\delta_{k}}}
, we obtain that with probability greater than
1-4d^{-1}

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)s\log d}{N_{k}}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\left(\frac{b_{k-1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2},

(A.15)

where we plug in
b_{k-1}=c_{3}(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/(2\beta)}
in the last step. This completes the proof for
k=2
.

By mathematical induction, assuming (
A.15
) holds for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
with probability greater than
1-2kd^{-1}
, we would like to prove (
A.15
) holds for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}
with probability greater than
1-2(k+1)d^{-1}
. Following the similar arguments, we can prove that
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k+1}\right)\asymp b_{k}
. Note that
\delta_{k+1}\asymp(\frac{b_{k}Ks\log d}{N})^{1/(2\beta+1)}
and
b_{k}=c_{3}(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/(2\beta)}
. As a result,
b_{k}\geq C\delta_{k+1}
, and by (
A.15
) it holds that
b_{k}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})}
with probability greater than
1-2kd^{-1}
. Finally, as shown in (
A.15
), we obtain that with probability greater than
1-2(k+1)d^{-1}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{b_{k}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2}.

This completes the proof for
k+1
. It is easily seen that, by the union bound argument, the event
\cap_{2\leq k\leq K}\{\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/2}\}
holds with probability greater than
1-2Kd^{-1}
.

Finally let’s consider the assumption (
3.13
).
By definition, we have
N_{k}=\sum_{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\in D_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left(R_{i}\right)=n\mathbb{E}(R_{i})/K=nc_{n,k}\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right)/K
. To ensure that
0<c_{n,k}\leq 1
, we require

N_{k}K\leq n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right),\ 2\leq k\leq K.

Note that
N_{k}K=N
,
with (
A.12
), it suffices to ensure that
N\leq Cb_{k-1}n
for some constant
C
and for all
2\leq k\leq K
. Some calculation yields (
3.13
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

By Theorem
1
, choosing
\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}
and

\lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}}
, yields that with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)},\ \|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}.

(A.16)

Similar as in Theorem
2
, we find
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)\asymp b_{1}
, leading to

\delta_{2}\asymp\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,2}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{s\log d~\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)}{N_{2}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{b_{1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}.

(A.17)

To invoke Theorem
1
for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
, we need to verify

b_{1}\geq C\delta_{2}~~\text{\ and\ }~~b_{1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}.

For simplicity, we denote
\Delta=\frac{Ks\log d}{N}
and
\alpha=\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}
. By (
A.17
) and (
A.16
), it suffices to verify

b_{1}\geq C^{\prime}\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}\text{\ and\ }b_{1}\geq C\Delta^{\alpha}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{1}{\Delta}\Big)}

(A.18)

for some constant
C,C^{\prime}
. For any fixed
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
, we have
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}
. To satisfy (
A.18
), we choose
b_{1}=C\Delta^{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}
for some constant
C
. With
\lambda_{2}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{1}\delta_{2}}}
, Theorem
1
implies, with probability greater than
1-4d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{b_{1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\Delta^{\alpha^{2}+\alpha}.

(A.19)

In the following, we will show that for any
2\leq k\leq\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\left(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}}\right)\rceil

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{\alpha-\alpha^{k}}{2(1-\alpha)}}\Delta^{(1-\alpha^{k})\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}:=r_{k},

(A.20)

holds with probability greater than
1-2kd^{-1}
. Note that
\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}})
is well defined for
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
. Clearly, (
A.20
) holds for
k=2
. Assuming (
A.20
) holds for
k-1
, it suffices to show (
A.20
) holds for
k
. Following the same argument above for
k=2
,
b_{k-1}
needs to satisfy

b_{k-1}\geq C^{\prime}\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}\text{\ and\ }b_{k-1}\geq Cr_{k-1}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{1}{\Delta}\Big)},

where
r_{k-1}
is given by (
A.20
). We note that for any
k\leq\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\left(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}}\right)\rceil
and for any fixed
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
,

(1-\alpha^{k-1})\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}=\left(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1}\right)\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}<\frac{1}{2\beta},

which implies

\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}=O\left(r_{k-1}\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}\right),

(A.21)

and therefore we can choose

\displaystyle b_{k-1}

\displaystyle\asymp r_{k-1}\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{\alpha-\alpha^{k-1}}{2(1-\alpha)}+\frac{1}{2}}\Delta^{(1-\alpha^{k-1})\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{(2\beta+1)(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})}{2(\beta+1)}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})}.

Similar to (
A.19
), we have with probability greater than
1-2kd^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(b_{k-1}\Delta\right)^{\alpha}\lesssim r_{k-1}^{\alpha}\Big\{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\Big\}^{\alpha/2}\Delta^{\alpha}=r_{k},

which completes the proof of (
A.20
).

For
k=K=\lceil\log_{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\left(1-\frac{\beta+1}{2\beta^{2}}\right)\rceil+1
, to satisfy

b_{K-1}\geq C^{\prime}\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}\text{\ and\ }b_{K-1}\geq Cr_{K-1}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{1}{\Delta}\Big)},

we set
b_{K-1}=c\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}
. To see this, note that
\Delta=o(1)
, the bound (
A.20
) holds for
r_{K-1}
, and thus for any fixed
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
, some calculation shows that

(1-\alpha^{K-1})\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}=\left(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{K-1}\right)\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}>\frac{1}{2\beta},

(A.22)

which implies

r_{K-1}\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}=O(\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}).

Applying Theorem
1
, we select

\delta_{K}\asymp\left(\frac{Kb_{K-1}s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},

and
\lambda_{K}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{K-1}\delta_{K}}}
,
to ensure, with probability greater than
1-2Kd^{-1}

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{K}\right)s\log d}{N_{K}}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{Kb_{K-1}s\log d}{N}\right)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/2}.

The result for
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}
follows similarly.

Finally, to ensure that
0<c_{n,k}\leq 1
for
2\leq k\leq K
, we require

N_{k}K\leq n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right),\ 2\leq k\leq K.

Note that
N_{k}K=N
. It suffices to ensure that
N\leq Cb_{k-1}n
for some constant
C
, which is implied by (
3.15
) for
2\leq k\leq K-1
and (
3.13
) for
k=K
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Following the proof of Theorem
2
we have by choosing
\delta_{1}=c_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}
and
\lambda_{1}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,1}K\log d}{n\delta_{1}}}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}}
, with probability greater than
1-2d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},~~\ \|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{s}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}.

(A.23)

Similar as in Theorem
2
, we find
\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)\asymp b_{1}
. Applying Theorem
1
with

\delta_{2}\asymp\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{nc_{n,2}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{s\log d\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)}{N_{2}}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\asymp\left(\frac{b_{1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},

(A.24)

and
\lambda_{2}=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{1}\delta_{2}}}
,
we have with probability greater than
1-4d^{-1}
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{K\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{2}\right)s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\lesssim\left(\frac{Kb_{1}s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}.

(A.25)

To satisfy

b_{1}\geq C\delta_{2}\text{\ and\ }b_{1}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)},

by (
A.24
) it suffices to verify

b_{1}\geq C^{\prime}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)}\text{\ and\ }b_{1}\geq C\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}

(A.26)

for some constants
C,C^{\prime}
. Since
{Ks\log d}=o(N)
, clearly we have

\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)}=O\left(\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}\right).

Therefore, to satisfy (
A.26
), we choose
b_{1}=C_{1}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)}
for some constant
C_{1}
. Then by
(
A.25
) and (
A.23
) we have

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2(2\beta+1)}}\Delta^{\frac{2(\beta+1)}{(2\beta+1)^{2}}},

where
\Delta=\frac{Ks\log d}{N}
. Using a similar mathematical induction argument, we can show that with probability greater than
1-2kd^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\Delta\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}\right)^{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}1/(2\beta+1)^{i}}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\Delta\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1})}\Delta^{1/(2\beta+1)^{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1})}\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k})},

(A.27)

for any
2\leq k\leq K
. Given
K=\lceil\log_{2\beta+1}(\log N)\rceil
, we have

\left(\frac{N}{Ks\log d}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta\cdot(2\beta+1)^{K}}}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
, hence
(
A.27
) with
k=K
can reduce to

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}},

and the result for
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}
follows similarly.
Since
\frac{1}{2}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1})<\frac{1}{2},

we can verify that

\Delta^{1/(2\beta)}\lesssim\left(\log(\frac{1}{\Delta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-2})}\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}(1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1})}
for all
2\leq k\leq K
.
Therefore, we choose

b_{k-1}=C_{1}\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-2}}{4\beta}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1-1/(2\beta+1)^{k-1}}{2\beta}},

and
\delta_{k}=C_{2}\left(\frac{b_{k-1}Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)}

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are some constants.
Finally, to ensure that
0<c_{n,k}\leq 1
, we require

N_{k}K\leq n\mathbb{P}\left((X,\bm{Z})\in S_{k}\right),\ 2\leq k\leq K-1.

Note that
N_{k}K=N
. It suffices to ensure that
N\leq Cb_{k-1}n
for some constant
C
, which is provided in (
3.21
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We first consider the case when
\beta>1
. The proof consists of the following two steps.

•

For any sampling method
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{N}(\mathcal{P}(\beta,s))
,
construct a set of hypotheses
\mathcal{H}=\left\{P_{j}(X,Y,\bm{Z})\right\}\subset\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
.

•

Apply Theorem 2.7 in
Tsybakov (2008)
by checking the following two conditions:

1.

\mathrm{KL}\left(f^{j}\|f^{0}\right)\leq\gamma\log|\mathcal{H}|
for some
\gamma\in(0,1/8)
, where
\mathrm{KL}\left(f^{j}\|f^{0}\right)
is the K-L divergence between probability measures
f^{j}
and
f^{0}
, and
f^{j}
is the probability measure of the random variables
\{O_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
under hypothesis
j
.

2.

For all
j\neq k
and
q=1,2,\left\|\bm{\theta}_{j}-\bm{\theta}_{k}\right\|_{q}\geq 2t
, where
t\asymp s^{\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}.

Given the set

\mathcal{M}=\left\{x\in\{0,1\}^{d}:\|x\|_{0}=s\right\},

there exists a subset
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
such that
\rho_{H}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)>s/16
for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime},x\neq x^{\prime}
and
\log\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|\geq c^{\prime}s\log\left(\frac{d}{s}\right)
, where
\rho_{H}
denotes the Hamming distance and
c^{\prime}
is some absolute constant. We let
\bm{\omega}_{0}=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and use
\bm{\omega}_{j}
to denote the elements in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for
j=1,\ldots,\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|
.

We construct
P_{j}(X,Y,\bm{Z})
as follows.
We choose weight functions such that
\gamma(1)=\gamma(-1)
.
For all
j=0,\ldots,\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|
, we assume
X
and each of
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{d}
follows a Uniform distribution on
[-1,1]
independently. For each
j=0,\ldots,\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|
, let

\displaystyle f_{j}(y=1\mid x,\bm{z})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\sigma}\left(x-c\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}\frac{\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}}{\sqrt{s}}\right),

(A.28)

\displaystyle f_{j}(y=-1\mid x,\bm{z})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2\sigma}\left(x-c\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}\frac{\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}}{\sqrt{s}}\right),

(A.29)

where
c
is some sufficiently small constant and
\sigma
is some sufficiently large constant. Under the assumption that
s(\frac{\log(d/s)}{N})^{1/2}=o(1)
, we can guarantee that
f_{j}(y=1\mid x,\bm{z})
and
f_{j}(y=-1\mid x,\bm{z})
are within
[0,1]
for any
(x,\bm{z})\in[-1,1]^{d+1}
, and thus are well defined.
By this construction we can conclude that
P_{0},\ldots,P_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}
are well defined probability measures. In the following we present
two lemmas which characterize two key properties of
P_{j}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
5
and the construction of
P_{j}=P_{j}\left(X,Y,\bm{Z}\right)
above, we have
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,L,p_{\min},p_{\max}),\forall j=0,\ldots,\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|
and (
3.2
) holds.

Proof.

By the construction of
P(X,Y,\bm{Z})
, denoting
\widetilde{c}=\frac{c}{\sqrt{s}}\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}
,
we have

\displaystyle f_{j}(y=1,\bm{z})=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}f_{j}(x,y=1,\bm{z})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}f_{j}(y=1\mid x,\bm{z})f(x)f(\bm{z})dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{d+1}}(1-\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}),

(A.30)

and
f_{j}(y=-1,\bm{z})=\frac{1}{2^{d+1}}(1+\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})
. Hence

f_{j}(x\mid y,\bm{z})=\frac{f_{j}(y\mid x,\bm{z})(1/2)^{d+1}}{f(y,\bm{z})}=\frac{1+\frac{y}{\sigma}(x-\widetilde{c}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})}{2(1-y\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{yx}{2\sigma(1-y\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})}

(A.31)

is
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
times differentiable w.r.t.
x
for any
y,\bm{z}
.
Now we check the condition in Definition
3.1
, i.e.,
f_{j}(x\mid y,\bm{z}),j=0,\ldots,\left|\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\right|
satisfies that

\left|f_{j}^{(l)}\left(x_{1}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f_{j}^{(l)}\left(x_{2}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right|\leq L\left|x_{1}-x_{2}\right|^{\beta-l}

(A.32)

for any
y\in\{-1,1\},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},x_{1},x_{2}
, and
L>0
is some constant. When
\beta=1
,
l=0
, by (
A.31
) we have

\left|f_{j}^{(0)}\left(x_{1}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f_{j}^{(0)}\left(x_{2}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right|=\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{2\sigma(1-y\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})}<\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sigma},

given
\sigma
sufficiently large. Then note that
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq 2
, therefore choosing
L=\frac{1}{\sigma}
we ensure that (
A.32
) is satisfied. For
\beta>1
,
l\geq 1
, we have
\left|f_{j}^{(l)}\left(x_{1}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)-f_{j}^{(l)}\left(x_{2}\mid y,\bm{z}\right)\right|=0
, hence (
A.32
) holds trivially. This means
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,L)
. Clearly,
f_{j}(x|\bm{z})=f_{j}(x)=1/2=p_{\min}
, and
f_{j}(x\mid y,\bm{z})\leq 1/2+1/\sigma=p_{\max}
. Thus,
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,L,p_{\min},p_{\max})
holds.

Now we check the condition (
3.2
), i.e.,

\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s^{\prime}}\frac{\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y\right)\bm{v}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq M_{1}.

(A.33)

By (
A.30
) we have

P_{j}(Y=1)=\int_{\bm{Z}}\frac{1}{2^{d+1}}(1-\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})d\bm{z}=\frac{1}{2},

hence
f_{j}(z\mid Y=y)=\frac{1}{2^{d}}(1-y\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})
.
We have

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y\right)\bm{v}=

\displaystyle\int(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{2}\frac{1}{2^{d}}(1-y\frac{\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T})\bm{v}-\frac{y\widetilde{c}}{\sigma}\int\frac{1}{2^{d}}(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{2}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}d\bm{z}.

Note that
\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\right)=1/3\mathbb{I}_{d}
and
\|\bm{\omega}_{j}\|_{0}=s
, hence

\displaystyle\Big|\int\frac{1}{2^{d}}(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{2}\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}d\bm{z}\Big|\leq s\mathbb{E}((\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z})^{2})\leq\frac{s}{3}\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2},

and

\displaystyle|\bm{v}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}\mid Y=y\right)\bm{v}|\leq\frac{1}{3}\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{c}{3\sigma}s\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/s)}{N}}\|\bm{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
s\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/s)}{N}}=o(1)
, (
A.33
) holds.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
5
and the construction of
P_{j}=P_{j}\left(X,Y,\bm{Z}\right)
above, the unique minimizer
\bm{\theta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the risk
R_{P_{j}}(\bm{\theta})
is

\bm{\theta}_{j}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }j=0,\\
\frac{c}{\sqrt{s}}\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}\bm{\omega}_{j}&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
c
is defined in (
A.28
).
In addition,
\|\bm{\theta}_{j}\|_{2}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
, and
\rho_{-}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\nabla^{2}R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla^{2}R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)\right)\leq\rho_{+}
for some constants
\rho_{+}\geq\rho_{-}>0
.

Proof.

Recall that
R_{j}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}_{j}\left[\gamma(Y)\left(1-\operatorname{sign}\left(Y\left(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\right)\right)\right)\right]
, and
\gamma(1)=\gamma(-1)=C
for some constant
C>0
,
the difference between
R_{j}(\bm{\theta})
and
R_{j}(\bm{\theta}_{j})
can be written as

\displaystyle R_{j}(\bm{\theta})-R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j}\left[\gamma(Y)Y\left(\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{Z}\right)-\operatorname{sign}\left(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathcal{G}}\operatorname{sign}\left(x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{E}_{j}[\gamma(Y)Y\mid x,\bm{z}]dP_{j;X,\bm{Z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2C\int_{\mathcal{G}}\operatorname{sign}\left(x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right)\mathbb{E}_{j}[Y\mid x,\bm{z}]dP_{j;X,\bm{Z}},

where

\mathcal{G}=\left\{(x,\bm{z})\mid\operatorname{sign}\left(x-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\right)\neq\operatorname{sign}\left(x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right)\right\},

P_{j;X,\bm{Z}}
is the joint distribution of
(X,\bm{Z})
under
P_{j}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j}[Y\mid x,\bm{z}]=

\displaystyle f_{j}(Y=1\mid x,\bm{z})-f_{j}(Y=-1\mid x,\bm{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}\left(x-c\left(\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}\right)^{1/2}\frac{\bm{\omega}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}\left(x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right),

(A.34)

\operatorname{sign}\left(\mathbb{E}_{j}[Y\mid x,\bm{z}]\right)=\operatorname{sign}\left(x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right).

Therefore,

R_{j}(\bm{\theta})-R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)=2C\int_{\mathcal{G}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{j}[Y\mid x,\bm{z}]\right|dP_{j;X,\bm{Z}}\geq 0,

(A.35)

hence
\bm{\theta}_{j}
is a minimizer of
R_{j}(\bm{\theta})
. In addition,
\|\bm{\theta}_{j}\|_{2}=c\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/s)}{N}}\|\bm{\omega}_{j}\|_{2}=c\sqrt{\frac{s\log(d/s)}{N}}=O(1)
.

Now we check the uniqueness. By (
A.34
) we have

R_{j}(\bm{\theta})-R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)=\frac{2C}{\sigma}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\left|x-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right|dP_{j;X,\bm{Z}}.

(A.36)

For any
\bm{\theta}\neq\bm{\theta}_{j}
, consider the set

\mathcal{G}_{\bm{z}}=\left\{\bm{z}:\left(\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)^{T}\bm{z}\neq 0,\left|\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\right|\leq 1,\left|\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right|\leq 1\right\}.

Note that there exists an open neighborhood in
\mathcal{G}_{\bm{z}}
, hence
\mathcal{G}_{\bm{z}}
has nonzero measure. Then
define

\bar{\mathcal{G}}=\left\{(x,\bm{z}):\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}<x<\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right.
or
\left.\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}<x<\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z}\in\mathcal{G}_{\bm{z}}\right\}
, we have
\bar{\mathcal{G}}\subset\mathcal{G}_{\bm{z}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{G}}
has nonzero measure as well. Therefore, (
A.36
) implies that
R_{j}(\bm{\theta})-R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)>0
, which completes the proof of the uniqueness.
Note that

\displaystyle\nabla R_{j}(\bm{\theta})=

\displaystyle\sum_{y=\pm 1}\gamma(y)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}yf\left(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y\right)f_{j}(\bm{z},y)d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{y=\pm 1}\gamma(y)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}yf_{j}\left(y\mid\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z}\right)f_{X,\bm{Z}}(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z},\bm{z})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{y=\pm 1}\gamma(y)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}y\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{y}{2\sigma}\left(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}-\bm{\theta}_{j}^{T}\bm{z}\right)\right)f_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{z})d\bm{z},

hence

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}R_{j}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{4\sigma}\sum_{y=\pm 1}\gamma(y)\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f_{\bm{Z}}(\bm{z})d\bm{z}=\frac{C}{2\sigma}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T})=\frac{C}{6\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{d},

and
\lambda_{\min}\left(\nabla^{2}R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)\right)=\lambda_{\max}\left(\nabla^{2}R_{j}\left(\bm{\theta}_{j}\right)\right)=\frac{C}{6\sigma}>0
. This completes the proof.
∎

For the second step of the proof, we check both the two conditions in the following.
Recall that
f^{j}
is the probability measure of the random variables
\{(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i},R_{i})^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}},(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},R_{i})^{\ind\{R_{i}=0\}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
under hypothesis
j
, and
\bar{H}_{i-1}=\{(X_{j},\bm{Z}_{j},Y_{j},R_{j})^{\ind\{R_{j}=1\}},(X_{j},\bm{Z}_{j},R_{j})^{\ind\{R_{j}=0\}}\}_{j=1}^{i-1}
. We have

\displaystyle f^{j}=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{f(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i}\mid\bar{H}_{i-1})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left\{f(R_{i}=0\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}\mid\bar{H}_{i-1})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=0\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{f(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}\cdot\left\{f(R_{i}=0\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=0\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{f(R_{i}=1\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}\left\{f(R_{i}=0\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})\right\}^{\ind\{R_{i}=0\}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \cdot f_{j}(Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i}),

where in the second last equation we used the fact that

R_{i}\perp Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1}
and
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},Y_{i})\perp\bar{H}_{i-1}
as specified in the class of sampling method in (
3.26
).
Since the sampling method
f(R_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})
and the joint distribution of
(X,\bm{Z})
keep invariant under different hypotheses, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\left(f^{j}\|f^{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\log\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{j}(Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}f_{j}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(Y_{i}\mid X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})^{\ind\{R_{i}=1\}}f_{0}(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})}\right]
