\displaystyle\leq c_{n,k}\int(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{4}\int K^{\prime 2}\left(\frac{x-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta_{k}}\right)f(x\mid\bm{z},y)\,dxf(\bm{z}\mid y)\,dz

\displaystyle=c_{n,k}\delta_{k}\int(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{4}\int K^{\prime 2}\left(u\right)f(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}+\delta_{k}u\mid\bm{z},y)\,duf(\bm{z}\mid y)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq c_{n,k}\delta_{k}||K^{\prime}||_{\infty}^{2}p_{max}\mathbb{E}(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{4}\leq c_{n,k}\delta_{k}.

Hence we could define
\rho^{2}=C_{2}c_{n,k}\delta_{k}
for some positive constant
C_{2}
.
Now for any sparse index set
S\subset\{1,\dots,d\}
,
|S|\leq Cs
, consider the sphere
\mathbb{S}_{S}=\bigl\{v:\ \|v\|_{2}=1,\ \mathrm{supp}(v)\subset S\bigr\}
.
Let
\mathcal{N}^{(\mathbb{S}_{S})}_{1/6}\subset\mathbb{S}_{S}
denote a
1/6
-net of
\mathbb{S}_{S}
such that
\forall\bm{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{S}
,

\exists\bm{u}\in\mathcal{N}^{(\mathbb{S}_{S})}_{1/6}
with
||\bm{u}-\bm{v}||\leq 1/6
. We know the cardinality of
\mathcal{N}^{(\mathbb{S}_{S})}_{1/6}
is no
larger than
18^{Cs}
and

\sup_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{S}}|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}|^{4}\ \leq\ c\max_{\bm{v}\in\mathcal{N}^{(S)}_{1/6}}|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}|^{4}

for some absolute constant
c
. Since there are no more than
\binom{d}{Cs}
such sparse index sets, we
know the cardinality of

\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcup_{S\subset\{1,\dots,d\},\ |S|\leq Cs}\ \mathcal{N}^{(\mathbb{S}_{S})}_{1/6}

is no larger than
(18d)^{Cs}
. Therefore we obtain

\|F\|_{P,2}^{2}=\ \|K^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}\!\left[\sup_{\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\ \|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq Cs}|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}|^{4}\right]\ \lesssim\ \|K^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{P}\!\left[\max_{\bm{v}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}}|\bm{v}^{T}\bm{Z}|^{4}\right]\ \lesssim\ \|K^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sigma^{4}\,(s\log d)^{2},

where the last inequality follows from the proof of Lemma 14.12 in
Bühlmann and Van De Geer (2011)
. With a similar derivation, we can
also show that

\|M\|_{P,2}^{2}=\ \bigl\|\max_{i\leq n}F(x_{i},y_{i},\bm{z}_{i},R_{i})\bigr\|_{P,2}^{2}\ \lesssim\ \|K^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sigma^{4}\,(s\log(d\vee n))^{2}.

Plugging the above results into Lemma
A.15
, we obtain when
a=d^{C_{1}}\geq n
or equivalently
\log d\gtrsim\log n
, with probability greater than
1-c(\log n)^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{||\bm{v}||_{2}=1,||\bm{v}||_{0}\leq Cs}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Omega,||\bm{\theta}||_{0}\leq Cs}\bigl|\bm{v}^{T}(\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{n}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{v}\bigr|

(A.113)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}s\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{3}}}+\frac{s\log(d\vee n_{k})}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}}\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}c_{n,k}.

∎

Lemma A.15
.

Under the setup above, suppose
F\geq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|f|
is a measurable envelope with
\|F\|_{P,2}<\infty
.
Let
M=\max_{i\leq n_{k}}F(x_{i},y_{i},\bm{z}_{i},R_{i})
and let
\rho^{2}
be any positive number such that

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f\|_{P,2}^{2}\leq\rho^{2}\leq\|F\|_{P,2}^{2}
.
Suppose there exist
a\geq e
and
\nu\geq 1
such that

\log\sup_{Q}N\!\bigl(\epsilon\|F\|_{Q,2},\,\mathcal{F},\,\|\cdot\|_{Q,2}\bigr)\;\leq\;\nu\log(a/\epsilon),\qquad\epsilon\in(0,1].

Then

\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\|G_{n}\|_{\mathcal{F}}\bigr]\;\leq\;K\Bigl(\rho\,\sqrt{\nu\log(a\|F\|_{P,2}/\rho)}\;+\;\frac{\nu\|M\|_{P,2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\,\log(a\|F\|_{P,2}/\rho)\Bigr).

Moreover, when
a\geq n
, with probability greater than
1-c(\log n)^{-1}
,

\|G_{n}\|_{\mathcal{F}}\ \leq\ K(q,c)\Bigl(\rho\,\sqrt{\nu\log(a\|F\|_{P,2}/\rho)}\;+\;\frac{\nu\|M\|_{P,2}}{\sqrt{n_{k}}}\,\log(a\|F\|_{P,2}/\rho)\Bigr),

where
K,K(q,c)
are absolute constants,
Q
is any finitely discrete probability measure,
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},||\cdot||)
is the covering number w.r.t the class
\mathcal{F}
and radius
\epsilon
, and
||f||_{P,p}=(\int|f|^{p}\,dP)^{1/p}
.

When
\beta>1
, we have similar results. To be specific, we also verify the Assumption
3.5
on the set
\Omega=\{\bm{\theta}:\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\delta_{k}\}
at the
k
th iteration for any
2\leq k\leq K
. We apply a proper kernel function
K
such that (i)
K
has bounded support on
[-1,1]
, (ii)
\|K\|_{\infty},\left\|K^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty},\left\|K^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\text{ and }\widetilde{K}=-\int K^{\prime}(t)tdt>0
are bounded above by universal constants. Then, it suffices to show that the following sparse eigenvalue condition holds w.h.p:

\displaystyle\rho_{\max}=\sup\left\{\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{v}:\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq Cs,\bm{\theta}\in\Omega,\|\bm{\theta}\|_{0}\leq Cs\right\}<C_{1}c_{n,k},

(A.114)

\displaystyle\rho_{\min}=\inf\left\{\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{v}:\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1,\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq Cs,\bm{\theta}\in\Omega,\|\bm{\theta}\|_{0}\leq Cs\right\}>C_{2}c_{n,k},

(A.115)

for some constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. We let
\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}=\mathbf{Cov}(\bm{Z})
and
g(\cdot)
be the p.d.f of the error
u
.
Denote
\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}\right]
as the population Hessian. We have the following lemmas and propositions:

Proposition A.5
.

Under the setup above, suppose
g(\cdot)
is an even function and satisfies the Lipschitz condition
|g(y)-g(x)|\leq L_{2}|x-y|
for any
-\frac{3\mu}{2}\leq x\leq y\leq-\frac{\mu}{2}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(t)(g(-\mu+t)-g(-\mu))\geq L_{1}|t|
. If we assume
\log d\gtrsim\log n
and

\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}s\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{3}}}+\frac{s\log(d\vee n_{k})}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}}\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)\lesssim c_{n,k},

(A.116)

then with probability greater than
1-c(\log n)^{-1}
, it holds that

\rho_{\min}\geq L_{1}\widetilde{K}c_{n,k}\lambda_{\min}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}\right)/2,~~\ \rho_{\max}\leq 2L_{2}\widetilde{K}c_{n,k}\lambda_{\max}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}\right).

Lemma A.16
.

Suppose
\widetilde{K}=-\int K^{\prime}(t)tdt>0
,
g(\cdot)
is an even function and satisfies the Lipschitz condition
|g(y)-g(x)|\leq L_{2}|x-y|
for any
-\frac{3\mu}{2}\leq x\leq y\leq-\frac{\mu}{2}
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(t)(g(-\mu+t)-g(-\mu))\geq L_{1}|t|
, then for all unit vector
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta^{*}})\bm{v}\leq L_{2}\widetilde{K}c_{n,k}\lambda_{\max}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}\right),\ \bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta^{*}})\bm{v}\geq L_{1}\widetilde{K}c_{n,k}\lambda_{\min}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{Z}}\right).

Lemma A.17
.

If we assume
\log d\gtrsim\log n
and

\sqrt{\frac{c_{n,k}s\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{3}}}+\frac{s\log(d\vee n_{k})}{n_{k}\delta_{k}^{2}}\log\left(\frac{(dslog{d)^{2}}}{c_{n,k}\delta_{k}}\right)\lesssim c_{n,k},

(A.117)

then with probability greater than
1-c(\log n_{k})^{-1}
, it holds that

\sup_{||\bm{v}||_{2}=1,||\bm{v}||_{0}\leq Cs}\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Omega,||\bm{\theta}||_{0}\leq Cs}\bigl|\bm{v}^{T}(\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{n}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{v}\bigr|\lesssim c_{n,k},

(A.118)

where
n_{k}=\frac{n}{K}
, and
c
is some positive constant.

We also verify the RSC/RSM condition on the population level, i.e. (
3.8
) for
\beta>1
and (
3.9
) for
0<\beta\leq 1
under mild conditions. For
\beta>1
, the result is presented in the following proposition.

Proposition A.6
.

If
c_{1}f(\bm{z})\leq f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=1)-f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=-1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=-1)\leq c_{2}f(\bm{z})
for some positive constant
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, then
c_{1}\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{\bm{z}})||\bm{v}||_{2}^{2}\leq\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}\leq c_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{\bm{z}})||\bm{v}||_{2}^{2}
.

Proof.

By definition (
1.2
)

\displaystyle R(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\gamma(Y)L_{01}\{Y(X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z})\}\right]

(A.119)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[L_{01}\{X-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\}\mid Y=1\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[L_{01}\{-X+\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\}\mid Y=-1\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X<\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\mid Y=1)+\mathbb{P}(X>\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}\mid Y=-1)

\displaystyle=\int\int_{-\infty}^{\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}}f(x\mid\bm{z},y=1)\ dxf(\bm{z}|y=1)\ d\bm{z}+\int\int_{\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}}^{\infty}f(x\mid\bm{z},y=-1)\ dxf(\bm{z}|y=-1)\ d\bm{z}.

Hence,

\nabla R(\bm{\theta})=\int\bm{z}f(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=1)\ d\bm{z}-\int\bm{z}f(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=-1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=-1)\ d\bm{z},

\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta})=\int\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=1)\ d\bm{z}-\int\bm{z}\bm{z}^{T}f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=-1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=-1)\ d\bm{z},

and we can get the quadratic form
\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}=\int(\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z})^{2}h(\bm{z})f(\bm{z})\ d\bm{z}
, where

c_{1}\leq h(\bm{z})=\left(f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=1)-f^{\prime}(\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}\mid\bm{z},y=-1)f(\bm{z}\mid y=-1)\right)/f(\bm{z})\leq c_{2}.

Thus,
c_{1}\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{\bm{z}})||\bm{v}||_{2}^{2}\leq\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}\leq c_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{\bm{z}})||\bm{v}||_{2}^{2}
.
∎

For the case
0<\beta\leq 1
, the result follows directly from Lemma
A.4
and the fact that
\nabla R(\bm{\theta}^{*})=0
.

A.8
Adaptation Theory

As shown in Section
3.2
, the optimality of the proposed estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{K}
critically depends on the choice of tuning parameters
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K},\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1},\{\delta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and the number of iterations
K
, which requires the knowledge of the smoothness parameter
\beta
and the sparsity parameter
s
. In this section, we propose Lepski’s methods
(
Lepskii, 1991
)
to achieve adaptation to unknown smoothness
\beta
and sparsity
s
respectively.

A.8.1
Adaptation to unknown sparsity
s

In this section, we assume that the smoothness parameter
\beta
is known and construct adaptive estimators
\widehat{s}
and
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}
, which achieves the optimal rate. Consider a discrete set
\mathcal{D}=\{s_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}
such that
s_{j}=2^{j}
and
2^{m}\leq d\leq 2^{m+1}
. If
\beta>1
, define the optimal rate
\psi(s)=(\frac{s\log d}{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and parameters
(b_{k-1}(s),\delta_{k}(s),\lambda_{k}(s))
to be those as in Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
in each iteration
k=1,\dots,K
, that is:
if
\beta>\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
, let

\delta_{1}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}(s)}},

and for
2\leq k\leq K
,

\delta_{k}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\ \lambda_{k}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}(s)\delta_{k}(s)}},b_{k-1}(s)=c_{3}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)}.

If
1<\beta\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}
, let

\delta_{1}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}(s)}},

for
2\leq k\leq K-1
,

\displaystyle\ b_{k-1}(s)=c_{3}\left(\log(\frac{N}{s\log d})\right)^{\frac{(2\beta+1)(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})}{2(\beta+1)}}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}(1-(\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1})^{k-1})},

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{b_{k-1}(s)s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{k}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}(s)\delta_{k}(s)}}

and

b_{K-1}(s)=c_{3}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\delta_{K}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta)},\ \lambda_{K}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}b_{K-1}(s)\delta_{K}(s)}}.

If
0<\beta\leq 1
, define the optimal rate
\psi(s)=\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}
and parameters
(b_{k-1}(s),\delta_{k}(s),\lambda_{k}(s))
to be those as in Theorem
4
in each iteration
k=1,\dots,K
, that is:

\delta_{1}(s)=c_{1}\left(\frac{s\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{1}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}(s)}},

and for
2\leq k\leq K
,

\displaystyle b_{k-1}(s)=

\displaystyle c_{3}\left(\log(\frac{N}{Ks\log d})\right)^{\frac{2\beta+1}{4\beta}[1-(\frac{1}{2\beta+1})^{k-1}]}\left(\frac{Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}[1-(\frac{1}{2\beta+1})^{k-1}]},

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(s)=

\displaystyle c_{1}\left(\frac{b_{k-1}(s)Ks\log d}{N}\right)^{1/(2\beta+1)},\ \lambda_{k}(s)=c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}b_{k-1}(s)\delta_{k}(s)}}.

Let
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s}
be the final estimator after
K
iterations of Algorithm
1
with parameter
(b_{k-1}(s),\delta_{k}(s),\lambda_{k}(s))
, where we note that the value of
K
only depends on
\beta
which is known in this case. Lepski’s estimator is defined as
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}
, where

\widehat{s}=\min_{s\in D^{\prime}}\{||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s^{\prime}}||_{2}\leq\bar{c}\psi(s^{\prime}),\forall s^{\prime}\geq s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\},

(A.120)

for some constant
\bar{c}>0
.
If the set to be minimized over is empty, we choose
\widehat{s}=2^{m}
. Let
s^{*}
be the true sparsity, then there must exists
j\in\{0,\dots,m\}
such that
s_{j-1}<s^{*}\leq s_{j}
.

Lemma A.18
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold, then with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(d)/d
, we have
\widehat{s}\leq s_{j}
.

Proof.

Define the sparsity class
C(s)=\{||\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{0}\leq s,||\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\leq C\}
for some constant
C>0
. Then, for any
s\geq s^{*}
,
C(s^{*})\subseteq C(s)
, which means Theorem
1
holds for any
s\geq s^{*}
. That is,
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\leq\psi(s)
holds with probability greater than
1-2K/d
for a single
s\geq s^{*}
. From the union bound, we know that with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(d)/d
,
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\leq\psi(s)
holds for each
s\geq s^{*},s\in\mathcal{D}
. We prove
\widehat{s}\leq s_{j}
by verifying that
s_{j}
is also in the set
\{||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s^{\prime}}||_{2}\leq\psi(s^{\prime}),\forall s^{\prime}\geq s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\}
. This is because by Theorem
1
for any
s^{\prime}\geq s_{j}\geq s^{*},s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
,

\displaystyle||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s_{j}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s^{\prime}}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s_{j}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}+||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s^{\prime}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\psi(s_{j})+C\psi(s^{\prime})\leq\bar{c}\psi(s^{\prime}),

with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(d)/d
. The last inequality is because the rate
\psi(s)
is a monotonically increasing function with respect to
s
and by choosing constant
\bar{c}
large enough.
By definition (
A.120
), we complete the proof.
∎

The following theorem shows that the proposed adaptive estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}
attains the optimal rate.

Theorem 11
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold, and
\log\frac{N}{Kd\log d}\geq\frac{\log 2}{1-c}
for some constant
c\in(0,1)
. With probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(d)/d
, we have

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\psi(s^{*}).

(A.121)

Proof.

From Lemma
A.18
, we know that with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(d)/d
,
\widehat{s}\leq s_{j}
. Thus, by definition of
\widehat{s}
and Theorem
1
, we have

\displaystyle||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{s}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s_{j}}||_{2}+||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{s_{j}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{c}\psi(s_{j})+C\psi(s_{j})\asymp\psi(s_{j}).

Thus, it suffices to show that
\psi(s_{j})\asymp\psi(s^{*})
. By the definition of
\psi(s)
and that
s_{j}=2s_{j-1}
, we obtain for
\beta>1

\frac{\psi(s_{j})}{\psi(s_{j-1})}=\frac{\left(s_{j}\log d/N\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\left(s_{j-1}\log d/N\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}=\sqrt{2},

and for
0<\beta\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\psi(s_{j})}{\psi(s_{j-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{Ks_{j}\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\left(\log\frac{N}{Ks_{j}\log d}\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}}{\left(\frac{Ks_{j-1}\log d}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\left(\log\frac{N}{Ks_{j-1}\log d}\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{s_{j}}{s_{j-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\left(\frac{\log\frac{N}{2Ks_{j-1}\log d}}{\log\frac{N}{Ks_{j-1}\log d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}\left(1-\frac{\log 2}{\log\frac{N}{Ks_{j-1}\log d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}\in[2^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}c,2^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}],

so
\psi(s_{j-1})\asymp\psi(s_{j})
. Since the rate
\psi(s)
is a monotonically increasing function with respect to
s
, we know that
\psi(s_{j-1})\leq\psi(s^{*})\leq\psi(s_{j})
, which implies
\psi(s_{j-1})\asymp\psi(s^{*})\asymp\psi(s_{j})
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.8.2
Adaptation to unknown smoothness
\beta

In this section, we assume that the sparsity
s
is known or can be specified based on domain knowledge. The goal is to construct an estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
, which does not require the knowledge of the smoothness parameter
\beta
. Recall that our algorithm involves a set of tuning parameters
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K},\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K-1},\{\delta_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
and the number of iterations
K
, and it produces a sequence of estimators
\{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
. To emphasize the dependence of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
on
\delta_{k}
, we denote the estimator by
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}}
.

For now, let us simply set
K=\lceil\log\log N\rceil
and
N_{k}=N/K
. For simplicity, we assume
n\gg N
, that is the total budget
N
is much smaller than the sample size
n
. Let
\Delta=\frac{s\log d}{N_{k}}
. Given any two points
\bm{\theta}
and
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}
in
\Omega_{k}
(defined in Assumption
3.5
), define

\rho_{k}(\delta_{k})=\frac{R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})-R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})^{T}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta})}{\|\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}}.

For
k\geq 2
, we set the size of the active set to be

\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})=C\Big(\delta_{k}\vee\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})}\sqrt{\frac{NKs\log d}{n^{2}\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}}\Big),

where
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}
is to be defined later and
C
is a large constant. Note that for
k=2
, we just set
\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})=1
in the above definition. Throughout the proof, the constant
C
may differ from line to line. By the proof of Theorem
2
, we set the sampling probability in (
2.6
) to be
\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})=\frac{CN}{n\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})}
for
k\geq 2
, and
\widehat{c}_{1}=c_{n,1}=\frac{N}{n}
which is independent of
\delta_{1}
. Moreover, set the regularization
parameter to be

\widehat{\lambda}_{k}(\delta_{k})=C\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}},

for
k\geq 2
, and
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}=C\sqrt{\frac{NK\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{1}}}
.
Now we are ready to introduce the Lepski’s method. Consider the discrete set
\mathcal{D}=\{1,\frac{1}{2},\dotso,\frac{1}{2^{m}}\}
such that
\frac{1}{2^{m}}\leq\frac{1}{n}<\frac{1}{2^{m-1}}.
The key idea is to choose the largest bandwidth in
\mathcal{D}
such that the bias is still dominated by the variance. Formally, the Lepski’s method selects

\widehat{\delta}_{1}=\max_{\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}}\bigg\{||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{\prime}}||_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1}^{\prime})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{\prime}}}~~\forall\delta_{1}^{\prime}\leq\delta_{1},\delta_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\bigg\},

(A.122)

and for
2\leq k\leq K

\widehat{\delta}_{k}=\max_{\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{D}}\bigg\{||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}^{\prime}}||_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{k}(\delta_{k}^{\prime})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k}^{\prime})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}^{\prime}}}~~\forall\delta_{k}^{\prime}\leq\delta_{k},\delta_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\bigg\},

(A.123)

for some constant
c>0
. If the set to be maximized over is empty, we choose
\widehat{\delta}_{k}=1/n
by default. We further define the oracles as

\delta^{*}_{1}=\max\bigg\{\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D},\delta_{1}^{\beta}\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}}\bigg\},

and for
k\geq 2

\delta^{*}_{k}=\max\bigg\{\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{D},\delta_{k}^{\beta}\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{\Delta\frac{\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})}{\delta_{k}}}\bigg\},

where
c^{\prime}>0
is a constant. Note that
\delta^{*}_{k}
is a random quantity as it depends
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}
via
\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})
.

Define the event
\mathcal{E}=\cap_{1\leq k\leq K}\mathcal{E}_{k}
, where for
k\geq 2

\mathcal{E}_{k}=\Big\{\|\nabla R^{D_{k}}_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{k},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})K\log d}{n\delta_{k}}},\forall\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{D}\Big\},

and

\mathcal{E}_{1}=\Big\{\|\nabla R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}K\log d}{n\delta_{1}}},\forall\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}\Big\}.

By the proof of Proposition
A.1
, we can easily prove the following lemma.

Lemma A.19
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold. The event
\mathcal{E}
holds with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(n)/d
.

Recall that

\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{y=\pm 1}\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}y\Big[\int_{X}\frac{1}{\delta}K\bigg(\frac{x-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\bigg)f(x|\bm{z},y)dx\Big]f(\bm{z}|y)d\bm{z}.

The following theorem shows the convergence rate of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{K}}
.

Theorem 12
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.5
hold,

\|\nabla_{S}\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{2}\geq C\delta^{\beta}
, and

\min\{|\bm{\theta}^{*}_{j}|:\bm{\theta}^{*}_{j}\neq 0\}\geq\begin{cases}C\delta^{*\beta}_{1},&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[6.0pt]
C\delta_{1}^{*}&\text{if }\beta\leq 1,\end{cases}

(A.124)

for some sufficiently large constant
C
. On the event
\mathcal{E}
, we have

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}},

(A.125)

and for
2\leq k\leq K

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}^{*}}}.

(A.126)

In particular, with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(n)/d

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{K}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\begin{cases}\Delta^{1/2},&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[6.0pt]
\log^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}(\frac{1}{\Delta})\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}&\text{if }1/2\leq\beta\leq 1.\end{cases}

(A.127)

To achieve adaptive estimation, we require two more conditions,
\|\nabla_{S}\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{2}\geq C\delta^{\beta}
, and (
A.124
). The first condition ensures that the approximation bias induced by the smoothed surrogate loss scales exactly as
\delta^{\beta}
. The condition is further discussed and verified in Section
A.8.3
. The second condition, known as the signal strength condition, appears to be necessary for deriving a sharp lower bound for Lasso-type estimators
(
Lounici et al., 2011
)
. This lower bound plays a key role in establishing the adaptivity property and can be also of interest in its own right.

This theorem shows that we can achieve adaptive estimation of
\bm{\theta}^{*}
without knowing the value of the smoothness parameter
\beta
. When
1/2\leq\beta\leq 1
, the rate of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{K}}
in (
A.127
) exactly matches the upper bound in Theorem
4
. However, when
\beta>1
, compared with Theorems
2
and
3
, the rate
\Delta^{1/2}=(\frac{Ks\log d}{N})^{1/2}
incurs an additional
K=\log\log N
factor. Indeed, the extra factor can be removed with a mild modification of our algorithm. Specifically, we allocate half of the label budget to the last batch, that is
N_{K}=N/2
, and
|D_{K}|=n/2
. For the first
K-1
batches, the label budget is allocated equally
N_{k}=N/(2K)
. The Lepski’s method remains the same except that in the last batch we define

\widehat{\delta}_{K}=\max_{\delta_{K}\in\mathcal{D}}\bigg\{||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{K}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{K}^{\prime}}||_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{K}(\delta_{K}^{\prime})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{K}(\delta_{K}^{\prime})s\log d}{n\delta_{K}^{\prime}}}~~\forall\delta_{K}^{\prime}\leq\delta_{K},\delta_{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\bigg\}.

Corollary A.1
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
12
, with probability greater than
1-2K\log_{2}(n)/d
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{K}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\begin{cases}\Big(\frac{s\log d}{N}\Big)^{1/2},&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[6.0pt]
\log^{\frac{1}{4\beta}}(\frac{1}{\Delta})\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}&\text{if }1/2\leq\beta\leq 1.\end{cases}

(A.128)

The proof of this corollary is nearly the same as the proof of Theorem
12
. The only difference is that the oracle
\delta^{*}_{K}
is defined as

\delta^{*}_{K}=\max\bigg\{\delta_{K}\in\mathcal{D},\delta_{K}^{\beta}\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{s\log d}{N}\cdot\frac{\widehat{b}_{K-1}(\delta_{K})}{\delta_{K}}}\bigg\}.

We omit the proof.

Proof of Theorem
12
.

By the definition of oracles, we know that

\delta_{1}^{*\beta}\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta^{*}_{1}}},~~\textrm{and}~~\delta_{k}^{*\beta}\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\frac{\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta^{*}_{k})}{\delta^{*}_{k}}}

for
k\geq 2
. In addition, Assumption
3.5
implies that
\rho_{k}(\delta_{k})\asymp\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})
for
\beta\geq 1
and
\rho_{k}(\delta_{k})\asymp\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})\delta_{k}^{\beta-1}
for
\beta<1
. Similar results hold for
k=1
if
\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})
is replaced with
\widehat{c}_{1}
. For notational simplicity, let

\psi_{1}(\delta)=\frac{1}{\rho_{1}(\delta)}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta}},~~~\psi_{k}(\delta)=\frac{1}{\rho_{k}(\delta)}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta)Ks\log d}{n\delta}},

for
k\geq 2
.

We now prove the theorem by induction. First, for
k=1
, by Lemma
A.20
, we have on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
,
\delta_{1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{1}}||_{2}+||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})+\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*}),

where we use the definition (
A.122
) and the proof of Theorem
1
shows that, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{1}}
with tuning parameters
\delta_{1}^{*}
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{1}
satisfies

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\widehat{c}_{1}}(\widehat{c}_{1}\delta_{1}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}})\asymp\delta_{1}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta^{*}_{1}}}\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta^{*}_{1}}}\asymp\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})

for
\beta\geq 1
and

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{(\delta^{*}_{1})^{1-\beta}}{\widehat{c}_{1}}(\widehat{c}_{1}\delta_{1}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}})\asymp\delta_{1}^{*}+\sqrt{\Delta\delta_{1}^{*(1-2\beta)}}\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\delta_{1}^{*(1-2\beta)}}\asymp\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})

for
\beta<1
. This completes the proof of (
A.125
).

Assuming that (
A.126
) holds for any
j\leq k-1
, our goal is to verify (
A.126
) holds for
k
. By Lemma
A.22
, we know on the event
\mathcal{E}
,
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}\leq C\delta_{k-1}^{*}
and
\delta_{k}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}||_{2}+||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})+||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2},

(A.129)

where we use the definition (
A.123
). To upper bound
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}
, we need to verify the proof of Theorem
1
is still valid with tuning parameters
\delta^{*}_{k}
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})
and
\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})
. The key is to verify the size of the active set satisfies (
3.11
). Clearly,
\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})\geq C\delta^{*}_{k}
and we can further show that

\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})\geq C\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})}\sqrt{\frac{NKs\log d}{n^{2}\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}}\geq C\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\Delta}}.

To see why the last step holds, we first use the assumption that (
A.126
) holds for
k-1
,

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\delta_{k-1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k-1}(\delta_{k-1}^{*})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k-1}^{*}}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\delta_{k-1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{NKs\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{k-1}^{*}\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\delta^{*}_{k-1})}}.

For
\beta\geq 1
, it reduces to

\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\delta_{k-1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{NKs\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{k-1}^{*}\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\delta^{*}_{k-1})}}\asymp\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\delta^{*}_{k-1})}{\delta^{*}_{k-1}}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})}{\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}}},

as
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}\leq C\delta_{k-1}^{*}
by Lemma
A.22
. Similarly, for
1/2\leq\beta<1
,

\frac{1}{\rho_{k-1}(\delta_{k-1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{NKs\log d}{n^{2}\delta_{k-1}^{*}\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\delta^{*}_{k-1})}}\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\delta^{*}_{k-1})\delta^{*(1-2\beta)}_{k-1}}\lesssim\sqrt{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-2}(\widehat{\delta}_{k-1})\widehat{\delta}^{(1-2\beta)}_{k-1}},

where in the last step we use both sides of the inequality
\delta_{k-1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}\leq C\delta_{k-1}^{*}
. Therefore, the condition (
3.11
) holds, and Theorem
1
implies

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})}(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})\delta_{k}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}^{*}}})\asymp\delta_{k}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\Delta\frac{\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})}{\delta^{*}_{k}}}\asymp\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})

for
\beta\geq 1
and

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta^{*}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\frac{(\delta^{*}_{k})^{1-\beta}}{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})}(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})\delta_{k}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*}_{k})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}^{*}}})\asymp\delta_{k}^{*}+\sqrt{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})\delta_{k}^{*(1-2\beta)}}\asymp\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})

for
\beta<1
. Together with (
A.129
), we obtain that
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})
. This completes the proof of (
A.126
).

Since the bound (
A.126
) also applies to
K
, what remains to do is to simplify the rate. However, the bound for
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}
depends on
\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta^{*}_{k})
and
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}
recursively. Thus, we need to understand the order of
\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}
, that is the order of
\delta_{k-1}^{*}
by Lemma
A.22
, from the first iteration. It is easily seen that
\delta_{1}^{*}\asymp\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}
. Therefore,

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{\frac{\beta\vee 1}{2\beta+1}}.

In the following, let us consider the three cases
\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
,
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
and
\beta\leq 1
separately. For
\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, (
A.126
) simplifies to

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\Delta\frac{\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k}^{*})}{\delta^{*}_{k}}}.

(A.130)

We start from
k=2
. Since
\delta_{1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{1}\leq C\delta_{1}^{*}
by Lemma
A.20
,
\widehat{b}_{1}(\delta_{2}^{*})\asymp\delta_{2}^{*}\vee\sqrt{\Delta^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}}
. In addition, the definition of
\delta_{2}^{*}
implies
\delta_{2}^{*(2\beta+1)}\asymp\Delta(\delta_{2}^{*}\vee\sqrt{\Delta^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\log\frac{1}{\Delta}})
. Given
\beta>(1+\sqrt{3})/2
, we can easily verify that
\delta_{2}^{*}
is the dominating term and it reduces
\delta_{2}^{*(2\beta+1)}\asymp\Delta\delta_{2}^{*}
, that is
\delta_{2}^{*}\asymp\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}
. By (
A.130
), we obtain

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{2}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Assuming that
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k-1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\delta_{k-1}^{*}\asymp\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}
, we can similarly show that
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\delta_{k}^{*}\asymp\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta}}
. The key is to verify
\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta^{*}_{k})\asymp\delta^{*}_{k}
, and therefore we can exactly replicate the analysis for
k=2
. The analysis for
1<\beta\leq(1+\sqrt{3})/2
is similar and is omitted. This completes the proof of the first part of (
A.127
).

Finally, for
\beta<1
, (
A.126
) simplifies to
||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\delta_{k}^{*}
. For
k=2
, note that
\widehat{b}_{1}(\delta_{2}^{*})\asymp\delta^{*}_{2}\vee\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{1/2}(\frac{1}{\Delta})
. Similarly, we can verify that
\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{1/2}(\frac{1}{\Delta})
is the dominating term, such that
\widehat{b}_{1}(\delta_{2}^{*})\asymp\Delta^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{1/2}(\frac{1}{\Delta})
. Then the definition of
\delta_{2}^{*}
implies
\delta_{2}^{*(2\beta+1)}\asymp\Delta\widehat{b}_{1}(\delta_{2}^{*})
, which reduces to
\delta_{2}^{*}\asymp\Delta^{(1+\frac{1}{2\beta+1})\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{\frac{1}{2(2\beta+1)}}(\frac{1}{\Delta})
. That is

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{2}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{(1+\frac{1}{2\beta+1})\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{\frac{1}{2(2\beta+1)}}(\frac{1}{\Delta}).

Finally, we can use induction method to show that

||\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\widehat{\delta}_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}||_{2}\lesssim\Delta^{(1+\frac{1}{2\beta+1}+...+\frac{1}{(2\beta+1)^{k-1}})\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log^{\frac{1}{2(2\beta+1)}(1+...+\frac{1}{(2\beta+1)^{k-2}})}(\frac{1}{\Delta}),

which agrees with (
3.24
). Following the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
4
, we complete the proof of the second part of (
A.127
).
∎

Lemma A.20
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
12
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, it holds that
\delta_{1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{1}\leq C\delta_{1}^{*}
for some sufficiently large constant
C
.

Proof of Lemma
A.20
.

Let us first prove
\delta_{1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{1}
. For any
\delta_{1}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{1}^{*}
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1})}\Big(\widehat{c}_{1}\delta_{1}^{\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}}}\Big)+\frac{1}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})}\Big(\widehat{c}_{1}\delta_{1}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\psi_{1}(\delta_{1})+\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*}),

(A.131)

where the last step holds by noticing the definition of
\delta_{1}^{*}
and
\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{1}^{*}
. Note that the notations
\psi_{1}(\delta)
and
\psi_{k}(\delta)
are defined in the proof of Theorem
12
. When
\beta\geq 1
,

\psi_{1}(\delta_{1})\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}}\geq\sqrt{\Delta\frac{1}{\delta_{1}^{*}}}\asymp\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*}),

and then (
A.131
) implies
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}\|_{2}\lesssim\psi_{1}(\delta_{1})
. When
1/2\leq\beta<1
,

\psi_{1}(\delta_{1})\asymp\sqrt{\Delta\delta_{1}^{(1-2\beta)}}\geq\sqrt{\Delta\delta_{1}^{*(1-2\beta)}}\asymp\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*}),

and then (
A.131
) implies
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}\|_{2}\lesssim\psi_{1}(\delta_{1})
. By the definition (
A.122
), we obtain that
\delta_{1}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{1}
.

Next, we prove
\widehat{\delta}_{1}\leq C\delta_{1}^{*}
by contradiction. Assume that for any constant
C_{a}
, we always have
\widehat{\delta}_{1}\geq C_{a}\delta_{1}^{*}
. Then there exists
\bar{\delta}_{1}\geq C_{a}\delta_{1}^{*}
and
\bar{\delta}_{1}\in\mathcal{D}
such that

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\bar{\delta}_{1}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}\|_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}}.

The triangle inequality yields,

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\bar{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\rho_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})}\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}Ks\log d}{n\delta_{1}^{*}}}+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{1}^{*}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime}\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*}).

(A.132)

However, Lemma
A.21
shows that
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\bar{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\bar{C}{\widehat{c}_{1}\bar{\delta}_{1}^{\beta}}/{\rho_{1}(\bar{\delta}_{1})}\geq C_{a}\bar{C}\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})
. Since
C_{a}
can be any constant, as long as we take
C_{a}>C^{\prime}/\bar{C}
, where
C^{\prime}
is the constant in (
A.132
), we will have
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\bar{\delta}_{1}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}>C^{\prime}\psi_{1}(\delta_{1}^{*})
, which leads to the contradiction with (
A.132
). This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.21
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
12
, for
\delta\geq C_{a}\delta_{1}^{*}
with
C_{a}
being a sufficiently large constant, we have
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\bar{C}{\widehat{c}_{1}\delta^{\beta}}/{\rho_{+}}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.21
.

By the KKT condition, we know that
\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})=\lambda_{1}\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{j})
for any
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{j}\neq 0
, which yields
|\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})|=\lambda_{1}
. Recall that we take
\lambda_{1}=C\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{1}K\log d}{n\delta_{1}}}
. With a sufficiently large constant
C
, we can claim that the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
implies
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}=\{\|\nabla R^{D_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla R_{\delta_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{1}/2\}
. Write

\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})=\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})+I_{j}+II_{j},

where
I_{j}=\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}_{1}^{*})-\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
and
II_{j}=\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla_{j}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})
. On the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}
,

\lambda_{1}/2\leq|\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})+II_{j}|\leq 3\lambda_{1}/2.

Then
\|\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})+II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}\leq 3\sqrt{\widehat{s}}\lambda_{1}/2
, where
\|v\|_{2,\widehat{S}}=\sqrt{\sum_{j:(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})_{j}\neq 0}v_{j}^{2}}
, and
\widehat{S}
is the support set of
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
with
\widehat{s}=|\widehat{S}|
. We further have

\|\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{2,\widehat{S}}\geq\|II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}-3\sqrt{\widehat{s}}\lambda_{1}/2.

(A.133)

Note that

\rho_{+}\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\|\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla_{j}R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{2}\geq\|\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})-\nabla R_{\delta}^{D_{1}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\|_{2,\widehat{S}}.

(A.134)

Combining (
A.133
) and (
A.134
), we obtain

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}(\|II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}-3\sqrt{\widehat{s}}\lambda_{1}/2)\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}(\|II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}-3C\sqrt{s}\lambda_{1}/2),

(A.135)

where the last step follows from
\widehat{s}\leq C^{2}s
by Lemma
A.9
. The next step to lower bound
\|II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}
. In the following we first prove that
S\subseteq\widehat{S}
. We prove by contradiction. Assume that there exists
j\in S\backslash\widehat{S}
. Then

|\bm{\theta}^{*}_{j}|=|(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*})_{j}|\leq\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\begin{cases}\delta^{\beta}+\sqrt{s}\lambda_{1}\lesssim\delta^{\beta},&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[6.0pt]
\delta+\sqrt{s}\lambda_{1}\delta^{1-\beta}\lesssim\delta&\text{if }\beta\leq 1,\end{cases}

where the last step holds because
\delta\geq C_{a}\delta_{1}^{*}
with
C_{a}
being a sufficiently large constant. In other words, when the bandwidth is sufficiently large, the bias can dominate the variance. This contradicts with the minimum signal strength condition (
A.124
).

Back to (
A.135
), we further have
\|II\|_{2,\widehat{S}}\geq\|II\|_{2,S}
, which yields

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}(\|II\|_{2,S}-3\sqrt{\widehat{s}}\lambda_{1}/2)\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{+}}(C^{\prime}\widehat{c}_{1}\delta^{\beta}-3C\sqrt{s}\lambda_{1}/2)\geq\frac{\bar{C}}{\rho_{+}}\widehat{c}_{1}\delta^{\beta}.

∎

Lemma A.22
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
12
, assume that (
A.126
) holds for
j\leq k-1
. On the event
\cap_{1\leq j\leq j}\mathcal{E}_{j}
, it holds that
\delta_{k}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{k}\leq C\delta_{k}^{*}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.22
.

Let us first prove
\delta_{k}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{k}
. For any
\delta_{k}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\delta_{k}\leq\delta_{k}^{*}
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}^{*}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}+\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}^{*}}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{k}(\delta_{k})}\Big(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})\delta_{k}^{\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}}}\Big)+\frac{1}{\rho_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})}\Big(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})\delta_{k}^{*\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta^{*})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}^{*}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k})+\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*}).

(A.136)

To see why the last step holds, we need to simplify the rate. Note that for
\beta\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})}(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})\delta_{k}^{\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}}})\asymp\delta_{k}^{\beta}+\sqrt{\Delta\frac{\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})}{\delta_{k}}}\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k})

and for
\beta<1

\frac{(\delta_{k})^{1-\beta}}{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})}(\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})\delta_{k}^{\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{c}_{k}(\delta_{k})Ks\log d}{n\delta_{k}}})\asymp\delta_{k}+\sqrt{\Delta\widehat{b}_{k-1}(\delta_{k})\delta_{k}^{(1-2\beta)}}\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}).

We can similarly verify that
\psi_{k}(\delta_{k})\geq C\psi_{k}(\delta_{k}^{*})
. Then (
A.136
) implies
\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{\delta_{k}^{*}}\|_{2}\lesssim\psi_{k}(\delta_{k})
and thus
\delta_{k}^{*}\leq\widehat{\delta}_{k}
. We can also prove
\widehat{\delta}_{1}\leq C\delta_{1}^{*}
by contradiction using the same argument. Indeed, we can similarly prove

\|\widehat{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}\geq\begin{cases}\bar{C}\delta_{k}^{\beta},&\text{if }\beta>1,\\[6.0pt]
\bar{C}\delta_{k}&\text{if }\beta\leq 1,\end{cases}

for
\delta_{k}\geq C_{a}\delta_{k}^{*}
. We omit the details.
∎

A.8.3
Remark on
\|\nabla_{S}\bar{R}_{\delta}(\theta^{*})\|_{2}\geq C\delta^{\beta}

Recall that

\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{y=\pm 1}\int_{\bm{Z}}\bm{z}y\Big[\int_{X}\frac{1}{\delta}K\bigg(\frac{x-\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{z}}{\delta}\bigg)f(x|\bm{z},y)dx\Big]f(\bm{z}|y)d\bm{z}.

For any
\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1
with
\|\bm{v}\|_{0}\leq s
, we have
\|\nabla_{S}\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\geq|\bm{v}^{T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta})|
. By the calculation in Proposition
A.2
,

\displaystyle|\bm{v}^{T}\nabla\bar{R}_{\delta}(\bm{\theta})|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int K(u)\frac{(u\delta)^{l}}{l!}\big(A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta)-A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z})\big)duf(z|y=1)dz

\displaystyle-\int\bm{v}^{T}\bm{z}\int K(u)\frac{(u\delta)^{l}}{l!}\big(A_{-1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta)-A_{-1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z})\big)duf(z|y=-1)dz\Big|,

where
A_{1}(x)=f^{(l)}(x|\bm{z},Y=1)
and
A_{-1}(x)=f^{(l)}(x|\bm{z},Y=-1)
. For simplicity, assume that
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}=1
, so we can take
\bm{v}=\bm{\theta}^{*}
. Thus, the condition
\|\nabla_{S}\bar{R}_{\delta}(\theta^{*})\|_{2}\geq C\delta^{\beta}
holds, if

\int\mathbb{E}\Big[(A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta)-A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}))\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}|Y=1\Big]K(u)u^{l}du\geq C\delta^{\beta-l},

(A.137)

and

-\int\mathbb{E}\Big[(A_{-1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+\tau u\delta)-A_{-1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}))\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}|Y=1\Big]K(u)u^{l}du\geq C\delta^{\beta-l}.

It remains to verify the above conditions case by case. Let us consider the following example to verify (
A.137
). Assume that
A_{1}(x)
is piecewise linear,

A_{1}(x)=C_{1}x,~~\textrm{for}~~x\geq 0,~~\textrm{and}~~A_{1}(x)=C_{2}x,~~\textrm{for}~~x<0,

where
C_{1}>C_{2}\geq 0
. Then we have
\beta=1
and
l=0
. Assume that the pdf of
N:=\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{z}
given
Y=1
, is continuous, upper bounded by a constant, and symmetric. The kernel function
K(\cdot)
is non-negative. To show (
A.137
), we have

\displaystyle\int\mathbb{E}\Big[(A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+u\delta)-A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}))\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}|Y=1\Big]K(u)du

\displaystyle=\int K(u)[I(u\geq 0)+I(u<0)]\mathbb{E}[(A_{1}(N+u\delta)-A_{1}(N))N|Y=1]du

\displaystyle=\int K(u)I(u\geq 0)\mathbb{E}[(A_{1}(N+u\delta)-A_{1}(N-u\delta))N|Y=1]du

where the last step holds by using the change of variable for
u<0
. Furthermore, given
u>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(A_{1}(N+u\delta)-A_{1}(N-u\delta))N|Y=1]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big\{I(N<-u\delta)NC_{2}u\delta+I(-u\delta<N<u\delta)N(C_{1}(u\delta+N)-C_{2}(N-u\delta))+I(N>u\delta)NC_{1}u\delta|Y=1\Big\}

\displaystyle=\delta u\Big\{C_{1}\mathbb{E}(I(N>u\delta)N|Y=1)+C_{2}\mathbb{E}(I(N<-u\delta)N|Y=1)\Big\}+O(\delta^{2})

\displaystyle=\delta u\Big\{C_{1}\mathbb{E}(I(N>0)N|Y=1)+C_{2}\mathbb{E}(I(N<0)N|Y=1)\Big\}+O(\delta^{2}).

Let
C:=\mathbb{E}(I(N>0)N|Y=1)
. Then

\displaystyle\int\mathbb{E}\Big[(A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}+u\delta)-A_{1}(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}))\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z}|Y=1\Big]K(u)du

\displaystyle=C(C_{1}-C_{2})\delta\int K(u)uI(u\geq 0)du+O(\delta^{2})\geq C^{\prime}\delta.

This verifies (
A.137
). Intuitively, condition (
A.137
) requires the non-smoothness of
A_{1}(x)
to be asymmetric, so that the bias from the positive and negative sides does not cancel each other out.

A.9
Binary Response Model with Diverging

\|\theta^{*}\|_{2}

For the binary response model, we can construct a counterexample that shows that the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix of
R(\bm{\theta}^{*})
at the true value
\bm{\theta}^{*}
are of order
1/\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
. When
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
is diverging, the eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})
shrink to 0 and thus the RSC condition would fail even if we only consider
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\theta}^{*}
. Thus, the statistical rate presented in Section
3
is generally not applicable to the case with diverging
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
. In the following, we provide the counterexample.

Recall that the binary response model takes the form
Y=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}(X-\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{Z}+u)
. For simplicity we assume
X\sim N(0,1)
,
\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\mathbf{I})
independent of
X
and
\gamma(y)\equiv 1
. In addition, we assume the error
u
is independent of
X
(but possibly depends on
\bm{Z}
), and the conditional density given
\bm{Z}=\bm{z}
, denoted by
f_{u|\bm{z}}(\cdot)
, is bounded by a constant for all
\bm{z}
. The median of
u
given
\bm{Z}
is 0.

By the definition of
R(\bm{\theta})
, it is easily shown that for any
\|\bm{v}\|_{2}=1

\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}=2\int(\bm{z}^{T}\bm{v})^{2}f_{u|\bm{z}}(0)\phi(\bm{\theta}^{*T}\bm{z})\phi(z_{1})...\phi(z_{n})d\bm{z},

where
\phi
is the pdf of
N(0,1)
. Since
f_{u|\bm{z}}(\cdot)
is upper bounded by a constant
C
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}

\displaystyle\leq 2C\int(\bm{z}^{T}\bm{v})^{2}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\exp(-\frac{\bm{z}^{T}(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{\theta}^{*T})\bm{z}}{2})d\bm{z}

\displaystyle=2C\frac{\bm{v}^{T}(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{\theta}^{*T})^{-1}\bm{v}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}|\mathbf{I}+\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{\theta}^{*T}|^{1/2}},

where in the last step we use the integral of a normal distribution with variance
(\mathbf{I}+\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{\theta}^{*T})^{-1}
. Since the matrix
\mathbf{I}+\bm{\theta}^{*}\bm{\theta}^{*T}
has
d-1
eigenvalues 1 and 1 eigenvalue
1+\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|^{2}_{2}
, we obtain that

\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}\leq\frac{2C}{(2\pi)^{1/2}(1+\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|^{2}_{2})^{1/2}}.

Thus, when
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
is diverging,
\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R(\bm{\theta}^{*})\bm{v}
is of order
1/\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
. Note that we can make the approximation error
\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}(R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-c_{n,k}R(\bm{\theta}^{*}))\bm{v}
ignorable with a small bandwidth
\delta_{k}
and the stochastic error
\bm{v}^{T}(\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*})-\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}(\bm{\theta}^{*}))\bm{v}
ignorable via concentration inequality. Thus, we can show that
\bm{v}^{T}\nabla^{2}R_{\delta_{k}}^{D_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\bm{v}
is also of order
c_{n,k}/\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
, violating our RSC condition (even at the true value
\bm{\theta}^{*}
) when
\|\bm{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}
is diverging.

A.10
Analysis of Region-based sampling

Recall that

Q_{i}=g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\ind\{f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})-g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\leq X_{i}\leq f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\}.

Since
\mathbb{E}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i})\leq N
holds and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R},\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x\mid\bm{z})\geq p_{\min}
for
P\in\mathcal{P}(\beta,s)
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}(g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})(g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})))=O(N).

Finally, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\cdot Q_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\cdot g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\ind\{f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})-g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\leq X_{i}\leq f_{i}(\bm{Z}_{i},\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p_{\max}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Z}_{i}\bm{Z}_{i}^{T}\cdot g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})(g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1}))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{\max}\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{3i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})(g_{1i}(\bar{H}_{i-1})+g_{2i}(\bar{H}_{i-1}))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(N)\cdot\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}\bm{Z}^{T}),

where we used the fact that
(X_{i},\bm{Z}_{i})\perp\bar{H}_{i-1}
. Since the sparse eigenvalue assumption (
3.2
) holds, the last condition in (
3.26
) holds as well.

A.12
Additional data analysis

In this section, we conduct an additional real data analysis using the MNIST handwritten digit dataset, a widely used benchmark in machine learning
(
LeCun et al., 1998
)
. The dataset contains 70,000 grayscale images of handwritten digits (0-9), each represented as a
28\times 28
pixel array.
To evaluate the effectiveness of our active sampling strategy, we consider a binary classification task using the digits ”3” and ”5”. We choose this pair because they are visually similar and relatively difficult to distinguish, making the classification task more challenging. To further increase the difficulty and to better reflect real-world scenarios in which one class may be underrepresented, we introduce class imbalance.
Specifically, we retain all images of the digit ”3” as the majority class (
n_{-}=7,141
) and randomly sample images of the digit ”5” to create a minority class. We consider two imbalance settings: (i) sampling probability
=10\%
, resulting in
n_{+}=793
images of the digit ”5”; and (ii) sampling probability
=20\%
, resulting in
n_{+}=1,785
images of the digit ”5”. The former setting represents a more difficult task, where we expect active sampling to provide greater improvement over passive sampling. The response variable is defined as
Y_{i}=-1
for digit ”3” and
Y_{i}=1
for digit ”5”. The sample size of the full dataset is
n=n_{-}+n_{+}
.

To incorporate the classification task into our framework, we choose a single pixel (pixel 350) as the primary measurement
X_{i}
, with the remaining 783 pixels plus an intercept term serving as covariates
\bm{Z}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{784}
. Similar to the setup in Section
6
, our goal is to estimate the optimal individualized threshold
\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
such that the digit class can be predicted based on whether the pixel intensity exceeds this threshold (
X_{i}\geq\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
) or falls below it (
X_{i}<\bm{\theta}^{T}\bm{Z}_{i}
).

We randomly split the dataset into 80% training set and 20% test set, with stratified sampling to preserve the class proportions. All pixel values are standardized to have zero mean and unit variance using statistics computed from the training set.
We compare the performance of our proposed two-step active subsampling method (Active PF) with the passive path-following method (Passive PF), where the labeled data are uniformly sampled from the training set. We consider label budgets of
N=250,500,1000
, and
2000
. For each budget, the experiment is repeated 10 times with different random seeds for sampling, while keeping the training/test split fixed.
Since the dataset is imbalanced, we use the F1 score as the primary evaluation metric, which accounts for both precision and recall and is more informative than accuracy in imbalanced settings. For completeness, we also report the test error.

The results are summarized in Figures
13
–
14
and Tables
3
–
4
. Figures
13
and
14
display the averaged F1 scores and test errors over 10 trials under the two imbalance settings. Recall that an F1 score of 1 corresponds to perfect classification performance. In both cases, our proposed active subsampling method consistently outperforms the passive path-following method across all label budgets. The improvement is more substantial in the more challenging setting with sampling probability
=10\%
, where the F1 score improves by approximately 0.03–0.05 compared to passive sampling. When the sampling probability is
20\%
, the improvement is smaller but still consistent, ranging from 0.01 to 0.02.
Similar patterns are observed for test errors. The active subsampling method achieves lower test errors across all budgets in both settings, with a larger performance gap in the more severely imbalanced case.

Moreover, Tables
3
and
4
show that the active subsampling method not only achieves higher averaged F1 scores but also exhibits smaller standard deviations in most cases, indicating more stable performance. For example, when the sampling probability is
10\%
and the label budget is
N=250
, the standard deviation of F1 score is 0.025 for Active PF compared to 0.045 for Passive PF. In conclusion, our proposed two-step active subsampling method consistently outperforms passive sampling across different budget levels and imbalance settings, with larger improvements in more challenging scenarios.
